              j) 6   k) 10   l) 8

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: use a floor number line the child can walk on, then a number track
  with numbered squares, and only then a line with marks. Keep to 0-20 and jumps
  of 1 and 2 at first. Harder: blank number lines with only two labels (find the
  missing labels), jumps of 10 and 100 from numbers like 386, mixed jumps (+100,
  +10, +1), and "how many jumps of 5 from 15 to 60?"


================================================================================
TOPIC 3.7 — ADDITION FACTS TO 20 (FLUENCY, STRATEGIES: DOUBLES, NEAR DOUBLES,
            MAKE-10)
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - Know all 121 addition facts with both numbers from 0 to 10 (from 0 + 0 up to
    10 + 10) quickly, without counting on fingers.
  - Adding 0 leaves a number the same; adding 1 or 2 is counting on 1 or 2;
    adding 10 to a one-digit number makes a teen number (10 + 7 = 17).
  - Doubles: 1 + 1 = 2 up to 10 + 10 = 20.
  - Near doubles: use a double and add 1 (7 + 8 = 7 + 7 + 1 = 15).
  - Make 10 (bridging): fill up to 10 first, then add the rest (8 + 5 = 8 + 2 +
    3 = 13).
  - Adding 9: add 10 and take 1 away (9 + 6 = 10 + 5 = 15).
  - Start from the bigger number (2 + 9 → 9 + 2) and choose the strategy that is
    quickest.
  - Use the addition square to see patterns: doubles on the diagonal, and every
    row goes up by 1.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. An egg tray has 2 rows with 6 eggs in each row. How many eggs are there?
        ● ● ● ● ● ●
        ● ● ● ● ● ●
    Answer: 12 eggs. 6 + 6 = 12.
    Teacher explains: When both numbers are the same, it is a double. Doubles
    are easy to remember because they come in pairs everywhere: two hands (5 + 5
    = 10), an egg tray (6 + 6 = 12), a spider's legs (4 + 4 = 8). Say all the
    doubles like a rhyme: 1 + 1 = 2, 2 + 2 = 4 ... 10 + 10 = 20. Now you try: 7
    + 7. (14)

D2. Riya picks 7 mangoes and Arjun picks 8 mangoes. How many mangoes did they
    pick?
    Answer: 15 mangoes. 7 + 8 = 15.
    Teacher explains: 7 and 8 are next-door numbers, so use a near double. 8 is
    7 and 1 more. So 7 + 8 = 7 + 7 + 1 = 14 + 1 = 15. We can also use 8 + 8 = 16
    and take 1 away: 15. Line up the mangoes in two rows to see that one row has
    one extra. Now you try: 6 + 7. (6 + 6 + 1 = 13)

D3. Dadi has 9 laddoos in one box and 5 laddoos in another. How many laddoos
    altogether? Use the ten-frame.
        [● ● ● ● ●]   [● ● ● ● ●]
        [● ● ● ● ○]   [○ ○ ○ ○ ○]
    Answer: 14 laddoos. 9 + 5 = 14.
    Teacher explains: The first ten-frame has 9 and one space is empty (○). The
    second has 5. Move one laddoo from the 5 to fill it: now we have a full 10
    and 4 left over. 10 + 4 = 14. This is making 10. With a 9 we always move
    just 1, so 9 + 5 is the same as 10 + 4. Now you try: 9 + 7. (10 + 6 = 16)

D4. Anu has 8 stickers. Her friend gives her 6 more. How many stickers does she
    have now? Show how you make 10 first.
    Answer: 14 stickers. 8 + 6 = 8 + 2 + 4 = 10 + 4 = 14.
    Teacher explains: Step 1: 8 needs 2 more to make 10 (ten-friends 8 and 2).
    Step 2: split the 6 into 2 and 4. Step 3: 8 + 2 = 10. Step 4: 10 + 4 = 14.
    Draw the split under the 6 like a little fork: 6 → 2 and 4. Now you try: 8 +
    5. (8 + 2 + 3 = 13)

D5. A box has 10 pencils and there are 7 loose pencils. How many pencils?
    Answer: 17 pencils. 10 + 7 = 17.
    Teacher explains: Adding a one-digit number to 10 is the easiest teen fact:
    the 1 in 17 is the ten and the 7 is the ones. No counting is needed! This is
    why making 10 is so helpful: once we reach 10, the rest is easy. Now you
    try: 10 + 4 (14), 3 + 10 (13).

D6. Sonu has to add 2 + 9. He starts at 2 and counts on 9 fingers. Is there a
    quicker way?
    Answer: Yes. Start from the bigger number: 9 + 2 = 11. Count on only 2: "10,
    11". Or use make-10: 9 + 1 + 1 = 11.
    Teacher explains: Adding numbers in the other order gives the same answer,
    so it is smart to start with the bigger number and count on the smaller one.
    Counting on 9 fingers is slow and it is easy to lose count. Now you try: 3 +
    8. (8 + 3 = 11)

D7. You roll two dice and get 6 and 5. What is the total?
        ● ● ● ● ● ●     ● ● ● ● ●
    Answer: 11. 6 + 5 = 11.
    Teacher explains: 6 and 5 are neighbours: 5 + 5 = 10, and 6 is one more than
    5, so 6 + 5 = 11. Or 6 + 6 = 12, and one less is 11. Play a dice game where
    each child says the total and the strategy used. Now you try: 4 + 5 (9), 6 +
    4 (10).

D8. In a cricket match Virat scored 9 runs in the first over and 8 runs in the
    second over. How many runs did he score?
    Answer: 17 runs. 9 + 8 = 17.
    Teacher explains: Here are three good ways. Adding 9: 10 + 8 = 18, then take
    1 away: 17. Near doubles: 8 + 8 = 16, and 9 is one more: 17. Make 10: 9 + 1
    + 7 = 17. Let the child choose and explain; all ways give 17. Now you try: 9
    + 6. (15)

D9. How many different ways can you work out 7 + 6? Show each one.
    Answer: 13. For example: 6 + 6 + 1 = 13 (double and one more); 7 + 7 − 1 =
    13 (double and one less); 7 + 3 + 3 = 13 (make 10).
    Teacher explains: There is no one right way. A child who knows many ways can
    check an answer by using a second way. Write the three ways on the board and
    ask the class to vote for their favourite. Praise clear explaining more than
    speed. Now you try: find two ways for 8 + 7. (8 + 8 − 1 = 15, 8 + 2 + 5 =
    15)

D10. Papa has ten 1-rupee coins in one pocket and ten in the other. How much
     money is that?
     Answer: 20 rupees. 10 + 10 = 20.
     Teacher explains: The biggest double in our facts is 10 + 10 = 20: two
     tens. Show two full ten-frames. Then look at doubles near it: 9 + 9 = 18
     (two less than 20) and 9 + 10 = 19. Now you try: 10 + 9 (19), 8 + 10 (18).

D11. Look at the addition square (0 to 10 across the top and down the side).
     Colour the doubles. What do you notice?
     Answer: The doubles 0, 2, 4, 6 ... 20 make a diagonal line from the top
     left corner to the bottom right corner. Near doubles sit right next to that
     line.
     Teacher explains: The square has 11 rows of 11 boxes, 121 boxes in all, one
     for every fact. Many are easy: adding 0 (a whole row and column), adding 1,
     adding 10. Colour the easy facts first, then the doubles, the near doubles
     and the make-10 facts. Only a few facts are left to practise hard, like 7 +
     5 and 8 + 4. Now you try: find 8 + 5 in the square. (13)

D12. Priya has 5 red beads and 8 blue beads on a string. How many beads are
     there? Use fives to help.
         ● ● ● ● ●   ○ ○ ○ ○ ○ ○ ○ ○
     Answer: 13 beads. 5 + 8 = 5 + 5 + 3 = 10 + 3 = 13.
     Teacher explains: 8 is 5 and 3. The 5 red beads and 5 of the blue beads
     make 10. Then 3 more is 13. Counting with fives is how a bead string
     (rekenrek) or our two hands work. Now you try: 5 + 7. (5 + 5 + 2 = 12)

M5. All the 8 facts. Adding 8: make 10 first. 8 needs 2 more to make 10 (8 + 5 =

M6. All the 7 facts. Adding 7: make 10 first. 7 needs 3 more to make 10 (7 + 6 =

M12. Quick-fire mixed facts, then missing numbers. Say which strategy you used!
     a) 9 + 7 = __    b) 6 + 8 = __    c) 5 + 7 = __    d) 8 + 3 = __
     e) 4 + 7 = __    f) 10 + 9 = __   g) 7 + 9 = __    h) 6 + 6 = __
     i) 3 + 9 = __    j) 8 + 6 = __    k) 5 + 6 = __    l) 2 + 9 = __
     m) 8 + __ = 15   n) 9 + __ = 13   o) 7 + __ = 16   p) 6 + __ = 11
     q) 5 + __ = 14   r) 4 + __ = 12   s) 9 + __ = 18   t) 7 + __ = 12
     u) 3 + __ = 11   v) 8 + __ = 17   w) 6 + __ = 14   x) 2 + __ = 10
     Answers: a) 16   b) 14   c) 12   d) 11   e) 11   f) 19   g) 16   h) 12
              i) 12   j) 14   k) 11   l) 11   m) 7   n) 4   o) 9   p) 5   q) 9
              r) 8   s) 9   t) 5   u) 8   v) 9   w) 8   x) 8

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: learn the facts in families, one strategy at a time: adding 0, 1,
  2 and 10 first, then doubles, then near doubles, then make-10 with 9 and 8.
  Use ten-frames and a bead string, and play dice games every day. Harder: timed
  quick-fire rounds (aim for about 3 seconds a fact), the full addition square
  from memory, and explaining two different strategies for the same fact.


================================================================================
TOPIC 3.8 — SUBTRACTION FACTS TO 20; RELATIONSHIP BETWEEN + AND −
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - Know all 121 subtraction facts that undo the addition facts: from 0 − 0 up
    to 20 − 10 (every c − b where both parts are 0 to 10).
  - Taking away 0 leaves a number the same (12 − 0 = 12); taking a number from
    itself leaves 0 (12 − 12 = 0).
  - Think addition: 13 − 9 = __ is the same question as 9 + __ = 13.
  - Count up from the smaller number, jumping to 10 first (12 − 8: 8 → 10 is 2,
    10 → 12 is 2, so 4).
  - Go back through 10 (14 − 6 = 14 − 4 − 2 = 8), take away 10 (18 − 10 = 8) and
    take away 9 (16 − 9 = 16 − 10 + 1 = 7).
  - Use doubles: 16 − 8 = 8 because 8 + 8 = 16.
  - Every addition fact gives subtraction facts: 7 + 5 = 12, so 12 − 5 = 7 and
    12 − 7 = 5.
  - Check a subtraction by adding.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. There are 15 laddoos on a plate. Ravi eats 6 of them. How many are left?
    Answer: 9 laddoos. 15 − 6 = 9.
    Teacher explains: Go back to 10 first. Take away 5 to get to 10 (15 − 5 =
    10). We still have to take away 1 more, because 6 = 5 + 1. 10 − 1 = 9. Show
    it on a double ten-frame: remove the 5 from the second frame, then 1 from
    the full frame. Now you try: 13 − 4. (13 − 3 − 1 = 9)

D2. Meena has 13 bangles. 9 of them are red and the rest are green. How many are
    green?
    Answer: 4 green bangles. 13 − 9 = 4.
    Teacher explains: Think addition: "9 and what make 13?" Count on from 9: 10
    is 1, and 3 more to 13, so 1 + 3 = 4. Check: 9 + 4 = 13. When the number
    taken away is big (like 9 or 8), thinking addition is much faster than
    counting back 9 times. Now you try: 12 − 9. (3)

D3. Priya's storybook has 14 pages. She has read 8 pages. How many pages are
    left to read?
        8 ━━→ 10 ━━━━━━→ 14
           +2       +4
    Answer: 6 pages. 14 − 8 = 6.
    Teacher explains: Count up from 8 to 14 on a number line. Jump to 10 first
    (2 jumps), then from 10 to 14 (4 jumps). Add the jumps: 2 + 4 = 6. Counting
    up is like the shopkeeper giving change. Now you try: 15 − 7. (7 → 10 is 3,
    10 → 15 is 5, so 8)

D4. Anu has 17 stickers and Ravi has 9 stickers. How many more stickers does Anu
    have?
    Answer: 8 more. 17 − 9 = 8.
    Teacher explains: "How many more" is a subtraction question: we find the
    difference. Line up the stickers in two rows, one under the other. 9
    stickers match; Anu has 8 extra. To calculate: take away 10 and add 1 back:
    17 − 10 = 7, 7 + 1 = 8. Now you try: 14 − 6. (8)

D5. Make a bead train with 7 red beads and 5 blue beads. Write one addition and
    two subtractions about it.
        ● ● ● ● ● ● ●   ○ ○ ○ ○ ○
    Answer: 7 + 5 = 12. 12 − 5 = 7 (take off the blue beads). 12 − 7 = 5 (take
    off the red beads).
    Teacher explains: The same three numbers, 7, 5 and 12, belong together.
    Adding puts the parts together to make the whole; subtracting takes one part
    away from the whole and leaves the other part. So if you know 7 + 5 = 12,
    you already know 12 − 5 and 12 − 7. Now you try: 9 + 6 = 15. (15 − 6 = 9, 15
    − 9 = 6)

D6. Papa has 18 rupees: a 10-rupee note and eight 1-rupee coins. He gives the
    10-rupee note to the vegetable seller. How much is left?
    Answer: 8 rupees. 18 − 10 = 8.
    Teacher explains: 18 is 1 ten and 8 ones. Taking away the ten leaves just
    the ones: 8. No counting needed! Every teen number minus 10 leaves its ones
    digit: 13 − 10 = 3, 19 − 10 = 9. And taking away the ones leaves 10: 18 − 8
    = 10. Now you try: 16 − 10 (6), 16 − 6 (10).

D7. There are 16 children in the room. 9 go out to play. How many are still in
    the room?
    Answer: 7 children. 16 − 9 = 7.
    Teacher explains: Taking away 9 is almost taking away 10. Take away 10: 16 −
    10 = 6. But we took away 1 too many, so give 1 back: 6 + 1 = 7. Check by
    adding: 9 + 7 = 16. Now you try: 14 − 9 (5), 11 − 9 (2).

D8. Arjun has 12 marbles. He gives away none. Then Asha has 12 marbles and gives
    away all of them. How many does each child have left?
    Answer: Arjun has 12 left (12 − 0 = 12). Asha has 0 left (12 − 12 = 0).
    Teacher explains: Taking away nothing does not change the number. Taking
    away everything leaves nothing. These two rules work for every number: 7 − 0
    = 7, 7 − 7 = 0, 20 − 0 = 20. Now you try: 15 − 0 (15), 9 − 9 (0).

D9. Rohan says 14 − 6 = 9. Check his answer with addition. Is he right?
    Answer: No. 6 + 9 = 15, not 14. The correct answer is 8, because 6 + 8 = 14.
    Teacher explains: Every subtraction can be checked by adding: the answer
    plus the number taken away must give the starting number. This is a great
    habit. Here 6 + 9 = 15, which is 1 too many, so the answer should be 1 less:
    8. Now you try: check 13 − 5 = 8. (5 + 8 = 13, so it is right)

D10. Dadi made 16 puris. Half of them are eaten, 8 puris. How many are left? Use
     a double.
     Answer: 8 puris. 16 − 8 = 8, because 8 + 8 = 16.
     Teacher explains: If you know the double 8 + 8 = 16, you know 16 − 8 = 8
     straight away. The doubles give easy subtractions: 12 − 6 = 6, 14 − 7 = 7,
     18 − 9 = 9, 20 − 10 = 10. Near doubles help too: 15 − 7 = 8 because 7 + 8 =
     15. Now you try: 14 − 7 (7), 13 − 6 (7).

D11. The auto-rickshaw stand has space for 20 autos. 11 autos are parked there.
     How many more autos can park?
     Answer: 9 more. 20 − 11 = 9.
     Teacher explains: Think addition: 11 + __ = 20. From 11 to 20 is 9 jumps
     (11 + 9 = 20). Or: 20 − 10 = 10, then take away 1 more: 9. Draw 20 boxes in
     two rows of 10, colour 11 and count the empty ones. Now you try: 20 − 12.
     (8)

D12. Kiran adds 6 to a number and gets 15. Then he takes away 6 again. What does
     he get? What was the number?
     Answer: He gets back to the number he started with: 9. 9 + 6 = 15 and 15 −
     6 = 9.
     Teacher explains: Subtraction undoes addition, and addition undoes
     subtraction. Adding 6 is a jump forward of 6 on the number line; taking
     away 6 is a jump back of 6 to the same place. That is why we can use
     addition facts to find subtraction facts. Note: we cannot take a bigger
     number away from a smaller one here (5 − 12 has no answer among our
     counting numbers), so in subtraction the order matters. Now you try: 8 + 7
     = 15, so 15 − 7 = __. (8)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Take away from 0, 1, 2, 3 and 4.
    a) 0 − 0 = __   b) 1 − 0 = __   c) 1 − 1 = __   d) 2 − 0 = __
    e) 2 − 1 = __   f) 2 − 2 = __   g) 3 − 0 = __   h) 3 − 1 = __
    i) 3 − 2 = __   j) 3 − 3 = __   k) 4 − 0 = __   l) 4 − 1 = __
    m) 4 − 2 = __   n) 4 − 3 = __   o) 4 − 4 = __
    Answers: a) 0   b) 1   c) 0   d) 2   e) 1   f) 0   g) 3   h) 2   i) 1   j) 0
             k) 4   l) 3   m) 2   n) 1   o) 0

M2. Take away from 5 and 6.
    a) 5 − 0 = __   b) 5 − 1 = __   c) 5 − 2 = __   d) 5 − 3 = __
    e) 5 − 4 = __   f) 5 − 5 = __   g) 6 − 0 = __   h) 6 − 1 = __
    i) 6 − 2 = __   j) 6 − 3 = __   k) 6 − 4 = __   l) 6 − 5 = __
    m) 6 − 6 = __
    Answers: a) 5   b) 4   c) 3   d) 2   e) 1   f) 0   g) 6   h) 5   i) 4   j) 3
             k) 2   l) 1   m) 0

M3. Take away from 7 and 8.
    a) 7 − 0 = __   b) 7 − 1 = __   c) 7 − 2 = __   d) 7 − 3 = __
    e) 7 − 4 = __   f) 7 − 5 = __   g) 7 − 6 = __   h) 7 − 7 = __
    i) 8 − 0 = __   j) 8 − 1 = __   k) 8 − 2 = __   l) 8 − 3 = __
    m) 8 − 4 = __   n) 8 − 5 = __   o) 8 − 6 = __   p) 8 − 7 = __
    q) 8 − 8 = __
    Answers: a) 7   b) 6   c) 5   d) 4   e) 3   f) 2   g) 1   h) 0   i) 8   j) 7
             k) 6   l) 5   m) 4   n) 3   o) 2   p) 1   q) 0

M4. Take away from 9. (Think: 9 is 1 less than 10.)
    a) 9 − 0 = __   b) 9 − 1 = __   c) 9 − 2 = __   d) 9 − 3 = __
    e) 9 − 4 = __   f) 9 − 5 = __   g) 9 − 6 = __   h) 9 − 7 = __
    i) 9 − 8 = __   j) 9 − 9 = __
    Answers: a) 9   b) 8   c) 7   d) 6   e) 5   f) 4   g) 3   h) 2   i) 1   j) 0

M5. Take away from 10. Use your ten-friends: 10 − 3 = 7 because 3 + 7 = 10.
    a) 10 − 0 = __    b) 10 − 1 = __    c) 10 − 2 = __    d) 10 − 3 = __
    e) 10 − 4 = __    f) 10 − 5 = __    g) 10 − 6 = __    h) 10 − 7 = __
    i) 10 − 8 = __    j) 10 − 9 = __    k) 10 − 10 = __
    Answers: a) 10   b) 9   c) 8   d) 7   e) 6   f) 5   g) 4   h) 3   i) 2
             j) 1   k) 0

M6. Take away from 11 and 12. (Think addition: 12 − 5 = __ because 5 + __ = 12.)
    a) 11 − 1 = __    b) 11 − 2 = __    c) 11 − 3 = __    d) 11 − 4 = __
    e) 11 − 5 = __    f) 11 − 6 = __    g) 11 − 7 = __    h) 11 − 8 = __
    i) 11 − 9 = __    j) 11 − 10 = __   k) 12 − 2 = __    l) 12 − 3 = __
    m) 12 − 4 = __    n) 12 − 5 = __    o) 12 − 6 = __    p) 12 − 7 = __
    q) 12 − 8 = __    r) 12 − 9 = __    s) 12 − 10 = __
    Answers: a) 10   b) 9   c) 8   d) 7   e) 6   f) 5   g) 4   h) 3   i) 2
             j) 1   k) 10   l) 9   m) 8   n) 7   o) 6   p) 5   q) 4   r) 3
             s) 2

M7. Take away from 13 and 14.
    a) 13 − 3 = __    b) 13 − 4 = __    c) 13 − 5 = __    d) 13 − 6 = __
    e) 13 − 7 = __    f) 13 − 8 = __    g) 13 − 9 = __    h) 13 − 10 = __
    i) 14 − 4 = __    j) 14 − 5 = __    k) 14 − 6 = __    l) 14 − 7 = __
    m) 14 − 8 = __    n) 14 − 9 = __    o) 14 − 10 = __
    Answers: a) 10   b) 9   c) 8   d) 7   e) 6   f) 5   g) 4   h) 3   i) 10
             j) 9   k) 8   l) 7   m) 6   n) 5   o) 4

M8. Take away from 15 to 20. The numbers taken away are big, so think addition!
    a) 15 − 5 = __    b) 15 − 6 = __    c) 15 − 7 = __    d) 15 − 8 = __
    e) 15 − 9 = __    f) 15 − 10 = __   g) 16 − 6 = __    h) 16 − 7 = __
    i) 16 − 8 = __    j) 16 − 9 = __    k) 16 − 10 = __   l) 17 − 7 = __
    m) 17 − 8 = __    n) 17 − 9 = __    o) 17 − 10 = __   p) 18 − 8 = __
    q) 18 − 9 = __    r) 18 − 10 = __   s) 19 − 9 = __    t) 19 − 10 = __
    u) 20 − 10 = __
    Answers: a) 10   b) 9   c) 8   d) 7   e) 6   f) 5   g) 10   h) 9   i) 8
             j) 7   k) 6   l) 10   m) 9   n) 8   o) 7   p) 10   q) 9   r) 8
             s) 10   t) 9   u) 10

M9. Think addition. Fill both blanks.
    a) 7 + __ = 15, so 15 − 7 = __   b) 8 + __ = 13, so 13 − 8 = __
    c) 9 + __ = 16, so 16 − 9 = __   d) 6 + __ = 14, so 14 − 6 = __
    e) 5 + __ = 12, so 12 − 5 = __   f) 8 + __ = 17, so 17 − 8 = __
    g) 4 + __ = 11, so 11 − 4 = __   h) 9 + __ = 18, so 18 − 9 = __
    i) 7 + __ = 12, so 12 − 7 = __   j) 6 + __ = 13, so 13 − 6 = __
    k) 3 + __ = 12, so 12 − 3 = __   l) 9 + __ = 14, so 14 − 9 = __
    m) 8 + __ = 11, so 11 − 8 = __   n) 7 + __ = 14, so 14 − 7 = __
    o) 5 + __ = 13, so 13 − 5 = __   p) 6 + __ = 15, so 15 − 6 = __
    Answers: a) 8, 8   b) 5, 5   c) 7, 7   d) 8, 8   e) 7, 7   f) 9, 9   g) 7, 7
             h) 9, 9   i) 5, 5   j) 7, 7   k) 9, 9   l) 5, 5   m) 3, 3   n) 7, 7
             o) 8, 8   p) 9, 9

M10. Go back to 10 first. Take away the ones to reach 10, then take away the
     rest. (14 − 6: 14 − 4 = 10, then 10 − 2 = 8.)
     a) 14 − 6 = 14 − 4 − __ = __   b) 13 − 5 = 13 − 3 − __ = __
     c) 15 − 7 = 15 − 5 − __ = __   d) 12 − 4 = 12 − 2 − __ = __
     e) 16 − 9 = 16 − 6 − __ = __   f) 11 − 3 = 11 − 1 − __ = __
     g) 17 − 8 = 17 − 7 − __ = __   h) 13 − 7 = 13 − 3 − __ = __
     i) 12 − 9 = 12 − 2 − __ = __   j) 15 − 8 = 15 − 5 − __ = __
     k) 14 − 5 = 14 − 4 − __ = __   l) 11 − 6 = 11 − 1 − __ = __
     m) 16 − 7 = 16 − 6 − __ = __   n) 13 − 9 = 13 − 3 − __ = __
     o) 12 − 3 = 12 − 2 − __ = __   p) 14 − 8 = 14 − 4 − __ = __
     Answers: a) 2, 8   b) 2, 8   c) 2, 8   d) 2, 8   e) 3, 7   f) 2, 8
              g) 1, 9   h) 4, 6   i) 7, 3   j) 3, 7   k) 1, 9   l) 5, 5
              m) 1, 9   n) 6, 4   o) 1, 9   p) 4, 6

M11. One addition fact gives two subtraction facts. Fill in the subtraction
     facts.
     a) 8 + 5 = 13  →  13 − 5 = __  and  13 − 8 = __
     b) 9 + 6 = 15  →  15 − 6 = __  and  15 − 9 = __
     c) 7 + 4 = 11  →  11 − 4 = __  and  11 − 7 = __
     d) 6 + 8 = 14  →  14 − 8 = __  and  14 − 6 = __
     e) 9 + 9 = 18  →  18 − 9 = __  and  18 − 9 = __
     f) 3 + 9 = 12  →  12 − 9 = __  and  12 − 3 = __
     g) 7 + 7 = 14  →  14 − 7 = __  and  14 − 7 = __
     h) 10 + 6 = 16  →  16 − 6 = __  and  16 − 10 = __
     i) 5 + 6 = 11  →  11 − 6 = __  and  11 − 5 = __
     j) 4 + 8 = 12  →  12 − 8 = __  and  12 − 4 = __
     k) 9 + 2 = 11  →  11 − 2 = __  and  11 − 9 = __
     l) 8 + 7 = 15  →  15 − 7 = __  and  15 − 8 = __
     Answers: a) 8, 5   b) 9, 6   c) 7, 4   d) 6, 8   e) 9, 9   f) 3, 9
              g) 7, 7   h) 10, 6   i) 5, 6   j) 4, 8   k) 9, 2   l) 8, 7

M12. Count UP from the smaller number to the bigger one. Jump to 10 first, then
     to the number. How far did you go?
     a) 12 − 9    b) 13 − 8    c) 11 − 7    d) 15 − 9    e) 14 − 10   f) 16 − 8
     g) 10 − 6    h) 17 − 9    i) 20 − 10   j) 18 − 9    k) 12 − 8    l) 19 − 10
     Answers: a) 9 → 10 (1), 10 → 12 (2); 1 + 2 = 3, so 12 − 9 = 3
              b) 8 → 10 (2), 10 → 13 (3); 2 + 3 = 5, so 13 − 8 = 5
              c) 7 → 10 (3), 10 → 11 (1); 3 + 1 = 4, so 11 − 7 = 4
              d) 9 → 10 (1), 10 → 15 (5); 1 + 5 = 6, so 15 − 9 = 6
              e) 10 → 14 (4), so 14 − 10 = 4
              f) 8 → 10 (2), 10 → 16 (6); 2 + 6 = 8, so 16 − 8 = 8
              g) 6 → 10 (4), so 10 − 6 = 4
              h) 9 → 10 (1), 10 → 17 (7); 1 + 7 = 8, so 17 − 9 = 8
              i) 10 → 20 (10), so 20 − 10 = 10
              j) 9 → 10 (1), 10 → 18 (8); 1 + 8 = 9, so 18 − 9 = 9
              k) 8 → 10 (2), 10 → 12 (2); 2 + 2 = 4, so 12 − 8 = 4
              l) 10 → 19 (9), so 19 − 10 = 9

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: always show the whole and the parts with counters or a bead
  string, and go through 10 on a double ten-frame. Learn subtraction facts with
  the matching addition facts, starting with −0, −1, −2, −10 and the doubles.
  Harder: quick-fire subtraction facts, give an addition fact and ask for both
  subtraction facts, and "check by adding" races where the child must find wrong
  answers.


================================================================================
TOPIC 3.9 — FACT FAMILIES AND MISSING-NUMBER PROBLEMS (3 + ? = 8)
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - Three numbers that belong together, such as 3, 5 and 8, make a FACT FAMILY:
    3 + 5 = 8, 5 + 3 = 8, 8 − 3 = 5, 8 − 5 = 3.
  - The biggest number is the WHOLE. It is the answer when we add, and the
    starting number when we take away. The two smaller numbers are the PARTS.
  - Write all four facts for any family within 20, and for families of tens (30,
    40, 70).
  - A double makes a family with only two facts: 6 + 6 = 12 and 12 − 6 = 6.
  - Find a missing number in ANY place: __ + 5 = 8, 3 + __ = 8, 8 − __ = 5,
    __ − 3 = 5, 8 = 3 + __. (Books also write the missing number as ? or as a
    box.)
  - Use the family to turn a hard question into an easy one: "3 + __ = 8" is the
    same as "8 − 3 = __".
  - Check the answer by putting it back into the number sentence.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Riya has 5 red bangles and 3 green bangles. She has 8 bangles in all. Write
    all the number facts you can make with 5, 3 and 8.
                8
               / \
              5   3
    Answer: 5 + 3 = 8, 3 + 5 = 8, 8 − 5 = 3, 8 − 3 = 5.
    Teacher explains: Draw a fact triangle with the whole (8) on top and the
    parts (5 and 3) at the bottom. Cover the 8 with your thumb: "5 and 3 make
    8", so we can add in both orders. Cover the 3: "8 take away 5 leaves 3".
    Cover the 5: "8 take away 3 leaves 5". Four facts, one family, like one
    family living in one house. Now let the child make a triangle for 4, 6 and
    10 and say all four facts.

D2. Ravi has 3 marbles. He wants to have 8 marbles. How many more marbles does
    he need?
    Answer: 5 more marbles. 3 + __ = 8, and the missing number is 5.
    Teacher explains: Ask: "Do we know the whole?" Yes, 8. "Do we know one
    part?" Yes, 3. So we need the other part. Two ways: count on from 3 to 8 on
    fingers ("4, 5, 6, 7, 8" is 5 fingers), or use the family: 8 − 3 = 5. Check
    by putting it back: 3 + 5 = 8. Then ask the child to try 6 + __ = 13 in the
    same way.

D3. There were some mangoes in a basket. Amma put in 6 more. Now there are 14
    mangoes. How many mangoes were in the basket at first?
    Answer: 8 mangoes. __ + 6 = 14, and the missing number is 8.
    Teacher explains: The START is missing. Children often add 14 and 6. Ask:
    "Is 20 more or less than 14?" Draw 14 mangoes and circle the 6 that Amma put
    in; the rest were there first. In the family 6, 8, 14 we can think
    14 − 6 = 8. Check: 8 + 6 = 14. Now you try: __ + 7 = 15.

D4. A bus had 15 people. At the stop some people got off. Now there are 9
    people. How many got off?
    Answer: 6 people. 15 − __ = 9, and the missing number is 6.
    Teacher explains: Turn it into an adding question: 9 + __ = 15. Count up
    from 9: "10" (1 step) and then 5 more steps to 15, so 6. The family is 6, 9
    and 15. Check: 15 − 6 = 9. Well done if you saw that adding and taking away
    are opposites!

D5. Meena gave 7 stickers to her friend. Now she has 6 stickers left. How many
    stickers did she have at the start?
    Answer: 13 stickers. __ − 7 = 6, and the missing number is 13.
    Teacher explains: The start is the WHOLE. The two parts are what she gave
    (7) and what is left (6). Put them back together: 7 + 6 = 13. Act it out:
    "Friend, please give back the 7 stickers!" Check: 13 − 7 = 6. Now try
    __ − 5 = 9.

D6. Sonu wrote the fact family for 4, 9 and 13 like this: 4 + 9 = 13,
    9 + 4 = 13, 13 − 4 = 9, 9 − 13 = 4. Is every fact right?
    Answer: No. The last fact should be 13 − 9 = 4.
    Teacher explains: When we take away, we always START with the whole, the
    biggest number. We cannot take 13 sweets from 9 sweets. Praise Sonu for
    getting three facts right, then ask the child to read all four facts aloud
    and point to the whole in each one.

D7. In a class there are 7 boys and 7 girls, 14 children in all. Why does this
    family have only two facts?
    Answer: 7 + 7 = 14 and 14 − 7 = 7. Turning the numbers round gives the same
    facts again.
    Teacher explains: In a double both parts are the same, so 7 + 7 turned round
    is still 7 + 7, and 14 − 7 is 7 either way. Doubles families are small
    families! Ask the child to write the family for 9, 9 and 18.

D8. Nani writes 12 = 5 + __ on the board. Sonu says, "That is wrong, the answer
    must come after the = sign." What is the missing number, and is Nani's sum
    wrong?
    Answer: 7. Nani is right: 12 = 5 + 7.
    Teacher explains: The = sign means "is the same as", like a balance that is
    level. It does not mean "the answer comes next". 12 on one side is the same
    as 5 and 7 on the other side. Read it both ways: "12 is the same as 5 plus
    7" and "5 plus 7 is the same as 12". Try: 15 = __ + 9.

D9. Our cricket team needs 20 runs to win. We have scored 13 runs. How many more
    runs do we need?
    Answer: 7 more runs. 13 + __ = 20, and the missing number is 7.
    Teacher explains: Count up from 13 to 20 in a friendly way: 13 to 20 is 7
    steps (14, 15, 16, 17, 18, 19, 20). Or use 3 + 7 = 10, so 13 + 7 = 20.
    Family: 7, 13, 20. Check: 20 − 13 = 7. Ask: "What if we had scored 16?" (4
    more.)

D10. Ravi has ₹30 in ₹10 notes. A toy costs ₹70. How much more money does he
     need?
     Answer: ₹40 more. 30 + __ = 70, and the missing number is 40.
     Teacher explains: Think in tens: 3 tens and how many tens make 7 tens? 4
     tens. The small family 3, 4, 7 helps with the big family 30, 40, 70:
     30 + 40 = 70, 40 + 30 = 70, 70 − 30 = 40, 70 − 40 = 30. Show it with ₹10
     notes. Now try 50 + __ = 90.

D11. Guess my number! "I think of a number. I take away 5 and I get 9." What is
     my number?
     Answer: 14. __ − 5 = 9, and 9 + 5 = 14.
     Teacher explains: Undo what was done: I took away 5, so we put the 5 back.
     9 + 5 = 14. Check: 14 − 5 = 9. Play this game often and let the child make
     up puzzles for you: "I add 6 and get 15" (9).

D12. Anu wants to make a fact family with 6 and 11 and one more number. What can
     the third number be? (There are two answers!)
     Answer: 5 (family 5, 6, 11) or 17 (family 6, 11, 17).
     Teacher explains: If 11 is the whole, the missing part is 11 − 6 = 5. If 6
     and 11 are both parts, the whole is 6 + 11 = 17. Both families are correct.
     Praise the child for finding both, and ask them to write all four facts for
     each family.

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Write the whole fact family (two add facts and two take-away facts) for each
    set of three numbers.
    a) 2, 5, 7     b) 3, 6, 9     c) 4, 7, 11    d) 5, 8, 13    e) 6, 9, 15
    f) 8, 9, 17    g) 3, 9, 12    h) 7, 6, 13    i) 4, 12, 16   j) 5, 14, 19
    k) 9, 11, 20   l) 8, 6, 14
    Answers:
      a) 2 + 5 = 7, 5 + 2 = 7, 7 − 2 = 5, 7 − 5 = 2
      b) 3 + 6 = 9, 6 + 3 = 9, 9 − 3 = 6, 9 − 6 = 3
      c) 4 + 7 = 11, 7 + 4 = 11, 11 − 4 = 7, 11 − 7 = 4
      d) 5 + 8 = 13, 8 + 5 = 13, 13 − 5 = 8, 13 − 8 = 5
      e) 6 + 9 = 15, 9 + 6 = 15, 15 − 6 = 9, 15 − 9 = 6
      f) 8 + 9 = 17, 9 + 8 = 17, 17 − 8 = 9, 17 − 9 = 8
      g) 3 + 9 = 12, 9 + 3 = 12, 12 − 3 = 9, 12 − 9 = 3
      h) 7 + 6 = 13, 6 + 7 = 13, 13 − 7 = 6, 13 − 6 = 7
      i) 4 + 12 = 16, 12 + 4 = 16, 16 − 4 = 12, 16 − 12 = 4
      j) 5 + 14 = 19, 14 + 5 = 19, 19 − 5 = 14, 19 − 14 = 5
      k) 9 + 11 = 20, 11 + 9 = 20, 20 − 9 = 11, 20 − 11 = 9
      l) 8 + 6 = 14, 6 + 8 = 14, 14 − 8 = 6, 14 − 6 = 8

M2. Write the whole family for each set. Careful: the last two are doubles, so
    they have only two facts.
    a) 10, 4, 14    b) 1, 17, 18    c) 8, 5, 13     d) 7, 9, 16
    e) 11, 6, 17    f) 12, 8, 20    g) 2, 13, 15    h) 9, 3, 12
    i) 6, 7, 13     j) 4, 15, 19    k) 10, 10, 20   l) 8, 8, 16
    Answers:
      a) 10 + 4 = 14, 4 + 10 = 14, 14 − 10 = 4, 14 − 4 = 10
      b) 1 + 17 = 18, 17 + 1 = 18, 18 − 1 = 17, 18 − 17 = 1
      c) 8 + 5 = 13, 5 + 8 = 13, 13 − 8 = 5, 13 − 5 = 8
      d) 7 + 9 = 16, 9 + 7 = 16, 16 − 7 = 9, 16 − 9 = 7
      e) 11 + 6 = 17, 6 + 11 = 17, 17 − 11 = 6, 17 − 6 = 11
      f) 12 + 8 = 20, 8 + 12 = 20, 20 − 12 = 8, 20 − 8 = 12
      g) 2 + 13 = 15, 13 + 2 = 15, 15 − 2 = 13, 15 − 13 = 2
      h) 9 + 3 = 12, 3 + 9 = 12, 12 − 9 = 3, 12 − 3 = 9
      i) 6 + 7 = 13, 7 + 6 = 13, 13 − 6 = 7, 13 − 7 = 6
      j) 4 + 15 = 19, 15 + 4 = 19, 19 − 4 = 15, 19 − 15 = 4
      k) 10 + 10 = 20, 20 − 10 = 10
      l) 8 + 8 = 16, 16 − 8 = 8

M3. The second part is missing. Find it.
    a) 3 + __ = 8     b) 6 + __ = 11    c) 9 + __ = 14    d) 4 + __ = 12
    e) 7 + __ = 15    f) 8 + __ = 17    g) 5 + __ = 13    h) 2 + __ = 11
    i) 10 + __ = 16   j) 6 + __ = 14    k) 9 + __ = 18    l) 7 + __ = 12
    m) 11 + __ = 20   n) 8 + __ = 8     o) 4 + __ = 13    p) 12 + __ = 19
    Answers: a) 5   b) 5   c) 5   d) 8   e) 8   f) 9   g) 8   h) 9   i) 6   j) 8
             k) 9   l) 5   m) 9   n) 0   o) 9   p) 7

M4. The first part is missing. Find it.
    a) __ + 5 = 8     b) __ + 6 = 14    c) __ + 9 = 13    d) __ + 7 = 16
    e) __ + 3 = 12    f) __ + 8 = 15    g) __ + 4 = 11    h) __ + 2 = 17
    i) __ + 9 = 20    j) __ + 5 = 12    k) __ + 6 = 13    l) __ + 8 = 18

M5. How many were taken away? Find the missing number.
    a) 8 − __ = 5     b) 13 − __ = 6    c) 15 − __ = 9    d) 11 − __ = 4
    e) 17 − __ = 8    f) 12 − __ = 7    g) 16 − __ = 10   h) 14 − __ = 5
    i) 20 − __ = 11   j) 18 − __ = 9    k) 10 − __ = 3    l) 19 − __ = 12

             k) 7   l) 7   m) 0   n) 9   o) 9   p) 0

M6. The start is missing. Find it.
    a) __ − 3 = 5     b) __ − 7 = 6     c) __ − 9 = 4     d) __ − 5 = 8
    e) __ − 8 = 8     f) __ − 6 = 9     g) __ − 4 = 7     h) __ − 11 = 3
    i) __ − 2 = 15    j) __ − 9 = 9     k) __ − 7 = 12    l) __ − 10 = 10

M7. The = sign is at the front. Find the missing number.
    a) 8 = 3 + __     b) 12 = __ + 4    c) 15 = 9 + __    d) 7 = __ + 7
    e) 11 = __ + 5    f) 14 = 6 + __    g) 5 = 13 − __    h) 9 = __ − 8
    i) 16 = 8 + __    j) 20 = __ + 13   k) 6 = 15 − __    l) 10 = __ − 7
    m) 17 = 9 + __    n) 13 = __ + 6    o) 4 = __ − 12    p) 18 = 20 − __
    Answers: a) 5   b) 8   c) 6   d) 0   e) 6   f) 8   g) 8   h) 17   i) 8
             j) 7   k) 9   l) 17   m) 8   n) 7   o) 16   p) 2

M8. Mixed missing numbers. The blank can be anywhere!
    a) 9 + __ = 16    b) __ − 6 = 7     c) 14 − __ = 8    d) __ + 8 = 17
    e) 19 = 12 + __   f) __ + 5 = 11    g) 13 − __ = 4    h) __ − 9 = 11
    i) 7 + __ = 15    j) 11 = __ − 6    k) 18 − __ = 9    l) __ + 3 = 20
    m) 6 + __ = 6     n) 15 = __ + 9    o) __ − 4 = 13    p) 12 − __ = 12
    q) 5 + __ = 14    r) 17 − __ = 8    s) __ + 7 = 13    t) 10 = __ − 10
    Answers: a) 7   b) 13   c) 6   d) 9   e) 7   f) 6   g) 9   h) 20   i) 8
             j) 17   k) 9   l) 17   m) 0   n) 6   o) 17   p) 0   q) 9   r) 9
             s) 6   t) 20

M9. Families of tens. Use the small fact to help (3 + 4 = 7, so 30 + 40 = 70).
    a) 30 + __ = 70    b) __ + 20 = 50    c) 80 − __ = 60    d) __ − 40 = 50
    e) 90 = 60 + __    f) 50 + __ = 100   g) __ + 70 = 80    h) 70 − __ = 30
    i) __ − 30 = 30    j) 100 − __ = 20   k) 40 + __ = 90    l) 60 = __ + 50
    m) __ + 80 = 100   n) 90 − __ = 10    o) __ − 50 = 40    p) 20 + __ = 20
    Answers: a) 40   b) 30   c) 20   d) 90   e) 30   f) 50   g) 10   h) 40
             i) 60   j) 80   k) 50   l) 10   m) 20   n) 80   o) 90   p) 0

M10. Use the first fact to fill in the second fact from the same family.
     a) 7 + 8 = 15, so 15 − 8 = __     b) 15 − 9 = 6, so 6 + 9 = __
     c) 5 + 12 = 17, so 17 − __ = 5    d) 8 + 4 = 12, so 12 − 4 = __
     e) 18 − 11 = 7, so 7 + 11 = __    f) 6 + 13 = 19, so 19 − __ = 6
     g) 9 + 9 = 18, so 18 − 9 = __     h) 18 − 4 = 14, so 14 + 4 = __
     i) 10 + 7 = 17, so 17 − __ = 10   j) 3 + 16 = 19, so 19 − 16 = __
     k) 19 − 8 = 11, so 11 + 8 = __    l) 5 + 7 = 12, so 12 − __ = 5
     Answers: a) 7   b) 15   c) 12   d) 8   e) 18   f) 13   g) 9   h) 18   i) 7
              j) 3   k) 19   l) 7

M11. Missing numbers with 0, doubles and the whole family. (In o) and p) both
     blanks are the same number.)
     a) __ + 0 = 9     b) 14 − __ = 14   c) 11 − __ = 0    d) __ − 0 = 16
     e) 0 + __ = 12    f) __ − 18 = 0    g) 7 + __ = 7     h) __ + 13 = 13
     i) 20 − __ = 20   j) __ − 5 = 0     k) 19 = 19 + __   l) 0 = 8 − __
     m) 8 + 8 = __     n) __ − 9 = 9     o) __ + __ = 14   p) __ + __ = 20
     Answers: a) 9   b) 0   c) 11   d) 16   e) 12   f) 18   g) 0   h) 0   i) 0
              j) 5   k) 0   l) 8   m) 16   n) 18   o) 7 and 7   p) 10 and 10

M12. True or false? If false, put it right.
     a) 6 + 9 = 15, so 15 − 9 = 6
     b) 9 − 13 = 4
     c) 12 − 5 = 7, so 7 + 5 = 12
     d) 8 + __ = 15, the blank is 23
     e) __ − 6 = 8, the blank is 2
     f) 11 − 4 = 7, so 11 − 7 = 4
     g) 16 = 9 + 7
     h) 3 + 14 = 17, so 14 − 3 = 17
     i) 40 + 50 = 90, so 90 − 40 = 50
     j) 13 − __ = 5, the blank is 8
     k) 10 = __ + 4, the blank is 14
     l) 7, 7, 14 has four different facts
     Answers: a) true   b) false: 13 − 9 = 4   c) true   d) false: it is 7
              e) false: it is 14   f) true   g) true   h) false: 17 − 3 = 14
              i) true   j) true   k) false: it is 6   l) false: only two

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: use a fact triangle card and cover one corner at a time; act out
  missing-start stories with real objects; stay within 10 until the child is
  sure, then move to 20. Harder: mix the position of the blank in every set, use
  families of tens and hundreds (300 + __ = 700), and let the child write their
  own missing-number puzzles for you to solve.


================================================================================
TOPIC 3.10 — COMMUTATIVE AND ASSOCIATIVE PROPERTIES OF ADDITION (informally)
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - ORDER does not matter when we add two numbers (the turn-around rule):
    4 + 9 = 9 + 4 = 13.
  - So we can always start with the BIGGER number and count on, and we only need
    to learn half of the facts.
  - GROUPING does not matter when we add three numbers: we can add any two
    first. Brackets ( ) show which pair we add first:
    (7 + 5) + 3 = 7 + (5 + 3) = 15.
  - Look for friendly pairs and add them first: pairs that make 10 (7 + 3),
    doubles (6 + 6), and pairs that make a tidy ten (25 + 5, 16 + 4).
  - Add 3, 4 or more numbers in a clever order; the total is always the same.
  - Taking away is DIFFERENT: 9 − 4 = 5, but 4 − 9 is not 5, and
    (10 − 4) − 2 = 4 while 10 − (4 − 2) = 8. Order and grouping DO matter in
    subtraction.
  - Use the turn-around to check an addition.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Look at this domino. It has 2 dots on one side and 9 dots on the other. Turn
    it round. How many dots now?
        [ ● ●  |  ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ]
        [ ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ●  |  ● ● ]
    Answer: 11 dots both ways. 2 + 9 = 11 and 9 + 2 = 11.
    Teacher explains: Turning the domino does not add or remove any dots, so the
    total stays the same. This is the turn-around rule for adding. It helps us:
    2 + 9 is slow if we count on 9 from 2, but 9 + 2 is quick: "9 ... 10, 11".
    Ask the child to find another domino and say both sums.

D2. Anu buys a pen for ₹8 and a notebook for ₹15. The shopkeeper adds 15 + 8 and
    Anu adds 8 + 15. Who is right?
    Answer: Both are right. 15 + 8 = 8 + 15 = ₹23.
    Teacher explains: The order of adding does not change the total. The
    shopkeeper's way is easier: start with the bigger number 15 and count on 8,
    or think 15 + 5 = 20 and 3 more is 23. Praise the child for noticing that
    the bigger number first is quicker.

D3. In three overs our team scored 7 runs, 5 runs and 3 runs. How many runs is
    that altogether?
    Answer: 15 runs. 7 + 5 + 3 = 15.
    Teacher explains: Look for a pair that makes 10. 7 and 3 are ten-friends!
    Add them first: 7 + 3 = 10, then 10 + 5 = 15. We may add the numbers in any
    order and the total stays the same. Now you try: 4 + 8 + 6.

D4. A basket has 6 apples, 9 bananas and 4 oranges. How many fruits are there?
    Answer: 19 fruits. (6 + 4) + 9 = 10 + 9 = 19.
    Teacher explains: Step 1: circle the ten-friends 6 and 4 (10). Step 2: add
    the 9 that is left: 10 + 9 = 19. Adding a number to 10 is very easy. If we
    went in order, 6 + 9 = 15 and 15 + 4 = 19: the same answer, but harder work.

D5. What do the brackets mean in (8 + 2) + 5 and in 8 + (2 + 5)? Work out both.
    Answer: (8 + 2) + 5 = 10 + 5 = 15 and 8 + (2 + 5) = 8 + 7 = 15. Both make
    15.
    Teacher explains: Brackets say "do me first". In the first sum we add 8 and
    2 first, in the second we add 2 and 5 first. The answer is the same: this is
    the grouping rule. Show it with three piles of counters: push two piles
    together first, then the third, and count. Then do it with a different pair.

D6. Ravi says, "10 − 3 is the same as 3 − 10, because order does not matter." Is
    he right?
    Answer: No. 10 − 3 = 7. We cannot take 10 sweets away from 3 sweets.
    Teacher explains: The turn-around rule works for ADDING only. Show 10
    counters and take away 3: 7 are left. Now show 3 counters and try to take
    away 10: we cannot! So in taking away, the order matters. Praise Ravi for
    remembering the rule; he just used it in the wrong place.

D7. Four boxes have 5, 8, 5 and 2 laddoos. How many laddoos are there in all?
    Answer: 20 laddoos. (5 + 5) + (8 + 2) = 10 + 10 = 20.
    Teacher explains: With four numbers, make two tens! 5 and 5 make 10; 8 and 2
    make 10; 10 and 10 make 20. Ask the child to cross off each pair as they use
    it so no number is missed or used twice.

D8. Dadaji walked 16 minutes in the morning, 9 minutes in the afternoon and 4
    minutes in the evening. How many minutes did he walk?
    Answer: 29 minutes. (16 + 4) + 9 = 20 + 9 = 29.
    Teacher explains: 6 and 4 make 10, so 16 and 4 make 20, a tidy ten. Add
    16 + 4 = 20 first, then 20 + 9 = 29. Friendly pairs are not only 10s: any
    pair that lands on a tens number is friendly.

D9. Sonu wants to add 3 + 17. He starts at 3 and counts on 17 fingers. Show him
    a quicker way.
    Answer: Turn it round: 17 + 3 = 20.
    Teacher explains: Start with the bigger number and count on the smaller one:
    "17 ... 18, 19, 20". Or see that 7 + 3 = 10, so 17 + 3 = 20. Encourage:
    "Before you add, look! Which number is bigger?" Now try 4 + 26.

D10. Meena added 26 + 13 and got 39. How can she check her answer?
     Answer: Add the other way round: 13 + 26. It is also 39, so she is right.
     Teacher explains: Because order does not matter, adding the numbers the
     other way round must give the same total. If it does not, one of the sums
     has a mistake. Checking with a turn-around is a good habit. Let the child
     check 34 + 45 = 79 the same way.

D11. Work out (10 − 4) − 2 and 10 − (4 − 2). Are they the same?
     Answer: No. (10 − 4) − 2 = 6 − 2 = 4, but 10 − (4 − 2) = 10 − 2 = 8.
     Teacher explains: The grouping rule works for adding, not for taking away.
     With 10 rupees: spend 4, then spend 2, and 4 are left. That is the correct
     meaning of 10 − 4 − 2: always take away from left to right. Let the child
     act it out with 10 coins.

D12. Riya has these coins in her piggy bank. How much money is there? Add in a
     clever order.
         ₹5   ₹2   ₹5   ₹1   ₹5   ₹2
     Answer: ₹20. (5 + 5) + (5 + 2 + 2 + 1) = 10 + 10 = 20.
     Teacher explains: Group the coins to make tens: two ₹5 coins make ₹10; the
     last ₹5 with ₹2, ₹2 and ₹1 makes another ₹10. Two tens are ₹20. Moving the
     coins about on the table shows the child that the money does not change
     when we change the order. Well done!

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Turn-arounds. Fill in the blank.
    a) 8 + 5 = 13, so 5 + 8 = __       b) 3 + 9 = 12, so 9 + 3 = __
    c) 7 + 6 = 13, so 6 + 7 = __       d) 2 + 14 = 16, so 14 + 2 = __
    e) 9 + 7 = 16, so 7 + 9 = __       f) 4 + 11 = 15, so 11 + 4 = __
    g) 6 + 8 = 14, so 8 + 6 = __       h) 12 + 5 = 17, so 5 + 12 = __
    i) 1 + 18 = 19, so 18 + 1 = __     j) 30 + 9 = 39, so 9 + 30 = __
    k) 20 + 50 = 70, so 50 + 20 = __   l) 15 + 4 = 19, so 4 + 15 = __
    m) 40 + 7 = 47, so 7 + 40 = __     n) 8 + 9 = 17, so 9 + 8 = __
    o) 25 + 30 = 55, so 30 + 25 = __   p) 5 + 60 = 65, so 60 + 5 = __
    Answers: a) 13   b) 12   c) 13   d) 16   e) 16   f) 15   g) 14   h) 17
             i) 19   j) 39   k) 70   l) 19   m) 47   n) 17   o) 55   p) 65

M2. Use the turn-around rule to find the missing number.
    a) 6 + 9 = 9 + __      b) __ + 7 = 7 + 12     c) 15 + 4 = __ + 15
    d) 8 + __ = 3 + 8      e) 11 + 5 = 5 + __     f) __ + 20 = 20 + 13
    g) 7 + 40 = 40 + __    h) 9 + 6 = __ + 9      i) 2 + 17 = 17 + __
    j) __ + 30 = 30 + 50   k) 14 + 3 = 3 + __     l) 5 + __ = 16 + 5
    m) 0 + 19 = 19 + __    n) 60 + 8 = __ + 60    o) __ + 9 = 9 + 9
    p) 24 + 5 = 5 + __
    Answers: a) 6   b) 12   c) 4   d) 3   e) 11   f) 13   g) 7   h) 6   i) 2
             j) 50   k) 14   l) 16   m) 0   n) 8   o) 9   p) 24

M3. Start with the BIGGER number. Write the turn-around and add.
    a) 2 + 9    b) 3 + 8    c) 1 + 7    d) 4 + 9    e) 2 + 16   f) 5 + 13
    g) 3 + 15   h) 1 + 19   i) 4 + 12   j) 6 + 23   k) 2 + 38   l) 3 + 46
    m) 5 + 34   n) 1 + 59   o) 4 + 65   p) 7 + 72
    Answers: a) 9 + 2 = 11   b) 8 + 3 = 11   c) 7 + 1 = 8   d) 9 + 4 = 13
             e) 16 + 2 = 18   f) 13 + 5 = 18   g) 15 + 3 = 18   h) 19 + 1 = 20
             i) 12 + 4 = 16   j) 23 + 6 = 29   k) 38 + 2 = 40   l) 46 + 3 = 49
             m) 34 + 5 = 39   n) 59 + 1 = 60   o) 65 + 4 = 69   p) 72 + 7 = 79

M4. Add the three numbers. First find the two that make 10.
    a) 7 + 5 + 3   b) 4 + 8 + 6   c) 9 + 2 + 1   d) 5 + 7 + 5   e) 3 + 6 + 7
    f) 8 + 4 + 2   g) 6 + 9 + 4   h) 2 + 5 + 8   i) 1 + 6 + 9   j) 7 + 7 + 3
    k) 5 + 8 + 5   l) 4 + 3 + 6   m) 9 + 8 + 1   n) 2 + 9 + 8   o) 3 + 4 + 7
    p) 6 + 1 + 4
    Answers: a) (7 + 3) + 5 = 15   b) (4 + 6) + 8 = 18   c) (9 + 1) + 2 = 12
             d) (5 + 5) + 7 = 17   e) (3 + 7) + 6 = 16   f) (8 + 2) + 4 = 14
             g) (6 + 4) + 9 = 19   h) (2 + 8) + 5 = 15   i) (1 + 9) + 6 = 16
             j) (7 + 3) + 7 = 17   k) (5 + 5) + 8 = 18   l) (4 + 6) + 3 = 13
             m) (9 + 1) + 8 = 18   n) (2 + 8) + 9 = 19   o) (3 + 7) + 4 = 14
             p) (6 + 4) + 1 = 11

M5. Work out both. Add the numbers in the brackets first. Is the answer the
    same?
    a) (6 + 4) + 7 = __ and 6 + (4 + 7) = __
    b) (3 + 7) + 8 = __ and 3 + (7 + 8) = __
    c) (5 + 5) + 9 = __ and 5 + (5 + 9) = __
    d) (8 + 2) + 6 = __ and 8 + (2 + 6) = __
    e) (9 + 1) + 4 = __ and 9 + (1 + 4) = __
    f) (2 + 8) + 3 = __ and 2 + (8 + 3) = __
    g) (7 + 3) + 5 = __ and 7 + (3 + 5) = __
    h) (4 + 6) + 6 = __ and 4 + (6 + 6) = __
    i) (5 + 9) + 1 = __ and 5 + (9 + 1) = __
    j) (8 + 7) + 3 = __ and 8 + (7 + 3) = __
    Answers: a) 17, 17, same   b) 18, 18, same   c) 19, 19, same
             d) 16, 16, same   e) 14, 14, same   f) 13, 13, same
             g) 15, 15, same   h) 16, 16, same   i) 15, 15, same
             j) 18, 18, same

M6. Add. First add the DOUBLE (the two numbers that are the same).
    a) 6 + 7 + 6   b) 8 + 3 + 8   c) 5 + 9 + 5   d) 7 + 4 + 7   e) 9 + 2 + 9
    f) 4 + 8 + 4   g) 3 + 6 + 3   h) 2 + 7 + 2   i) 8 + 5 + 8   j) 6 + 1 + 6
    k) 9 + 9 + 1   l) 7 + 7 + 5
    Answers: a) (6 + 6) + 7 = 19   b) (8 + 8) + 3 = 19   c) (5 + 5) + 9 = 19
             d) (7 + 7) + 4 = 18   e) (9 + 9) + 2 = 20   f) (4 + 4) + 8 = 16
             g) (3 + 3) + 6 = 12   h) (2 + 2) + 7 = 11   i) (8 + 8) + 5 = 21
             j) (6 + 6) + 1 = 13   k) (9 + 9) + 1 = 19   l) (7 + 7) + 5 = 19

M7. Add the four numbers by making two tens.
    a) 3 + 5 + 7 + 5   b) 2 + 9 + 8 + 1   c) 6 + 3 + 4 + 7   d) 9 + 5 + 1 + 5
    e) 4 + 4 + 6 + 6   f) 8 + 7 + 2 + 3   g) 1 + 6 + 9 + 4   h) 5 + 2 + 5 + 8
    i) 7 + 6 + 3 + 4   j) 2 + 3 + 8 + 7   k) 9 + 4 + 6 + 1   l) 5 + 5 + 5 + 5
    Answers:
      a) (3 + 7) + (5 + 5) = 20
      b) (2 + 8) + (9 + 1) = 20
      c) (6 + 4) + (3 + 7) = 20
      d) (9 + 1) + (5 + 5) = 20
      e) (4 + 6) + (4 + 6) = 20
      f) (8 + 2) + (7 + 3) = 20
      g) (1 + 9) + (6 + 4) = 20
      h) (5 + 5) + (2 + 8) = 20
      i) (7 + 3) + (6 + 4) = 20
      j) (2 + 8) + (3 + 7) = 20
      k) (9 + 1) + (4 + 6) = 20
      l) (5 + 5) + (5 + 5) = 20

M8. Add. Look for a pair that makes a tidy tens number first.
    a) 25 + 8 + 5    b) 16 + 7 + 4    c) 18 + 9 + 2    d) 35 + 6 + 5
    e) 30 + 9 + 20   f) 13 + 8 + 7    g) 44 + 5 + 6    h) 27 + 9 + 3
    i) 50 + 7 + 50   j) 19 + 6 + 1    k) 22 + 4 + 8    l) 45 + 3 + 5

      i) (50 + 50) + 7 = 107
      j) (19 + 1) + 6 = 26
      k) (22 + 8) + 4 = 34
      l) (45 + 5) + 3 = 53

M9. True or false?
    a) 9 + 6 = 6 + 9               b) 9 − 6 = 6 − 9
    c) (5 + 3) + 2 = 5 + (3 + 2)   d) (9 − 5) − 2 = 9 − (5 − 2)
    e) 12 + 0 = 0 + 12             f) 14 + 5 = 15 + 4
    g) 8 − 0 = 0 − 8               h) 7 + 8 + 3 = 3 + 7 + 8
    i) 20 − 10 = 10 − 20           j) (6 + 4) + 5 = 6 + (4 + 5)
    k) 13 + 6 = 13 + 7             l) 40 + 30 = 30 + 40
    Answers: a) true   b) false   c) true   d) false (2 and 6)   e) true
             f) true   g) false   h) true   i) false   j) true
             k) false (19 and 20)   l) true

M10. Fill in the missing number. Do not add everything; use the rules!
     a) (4 + 5) + 6 = 4 + (__ + 6)     b) 7 + 8 + 3 = 7 + 3 + __
     c) 13 + 9 + 7 = 20 + __           d) (2 + 9) + 8 = __ + 9
     e) 6 + 5 + 4 = 10 + __            f) (12 + 8) + 5 = 12 + (8 + __)
     g) 9 + 7 + 1 = __ + 7             h) 3 + 8 + 2 + 7 = 10 + __
     i) 15 + 6 + 5 = __ + 6            j) (8 + 7) + 3 = 8 + (__ + 3)
     k) 1 + 16 + 4 = 1 + __            l) 25 + 9 + 5 = 30 + __
     Answers: a) 5   b) 8   c) 9   d) 10   e) 5   f) 5   g) 10   h) 10   i) 20
              j) 7   k) 20   l) 9

M11. Long sums. Pair up numbers to make tens, then add the tens.
     a) 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 + 5 + 6 + 7 + 8 + 9
     b) 2 + 4 + 6 + 8
     c) 1 + 3 + 5 + 7 + 9
     d) 9 + 8 + 7 + 3 + 2 + 1
     e) 5 + 5 + 5 + 5 + 5 + 5
     f) 6 + 7 + 8 + 4 + 3 + 2
     g) 10 + 20 + 30 + 40
     h) 11 + 12 + 13 + 17 + 18 + 19
     Answers: a) 45   b) 20   c) 25   d) 30   e) 30   f) 30   g) 100   h) 90

M12. Taking away is different! Work out both. Are they the same?
     a) (12 − 5) − 3 = __ and 12 − (5 − 3) = __
     b) (15 − 6) − 2 = __ and 15 − (6 − 2) = __
     c) (20 − 8) − 4 = __ and 20 − (8 − 4) = __
     d) (18 − 9) − 5 = __ and 18 − (9 − 5) = __
     e) (10 − 6) − 3 = __ and 10 − (6 − 3) = __
     f) (16 − 7) − 1 = __ and 16 − (7 − 1) = __
     g) (14 − 4) − 2 = __ and 14 − (4 − 2) = __
     h) (19 − 10) − 5 = __ and 19 − (10 − 5) = __
     i) (17 − 8) − 6 = __ and 17 − (8 − 6) = __
     j) (13 − 4) − 2 = __ and 13 − (4 − 2) = __
     Answers:
       a) 4, 10, not the same
       b) 7, 11, not the same
       c) 8, 16, not the same
       d) 4, 14, not the same
       e) 1, 7, not the same
       f) 8, 10, not the same
       g) 8, 12, not the same
       h) 4, 14, not the same
       i) 3, 15, not the same
       j) 7, 11, not the same

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: use dominoes, bead strings and piles of counters so the child SEES
  that the total does not change; practise one rule at a time (turn-arounds
  first, then three numbers with an obvious 10). Harder: add long lists of
  numbers in the quickest order, explain why (9 − 5) − 2 and 9 − (5 − 2) are
  different, and invent sums where a clever order saves a lot of work (adding
  all the numbers from 1 to 9).


================================================================================
TOPIC 3.11 — DOUBLING AND HALVING; ADDING AND MULTIPLYING WITH 0 AND 1
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - DOUBLE means two times as many: add a number to itself. Double 7 is
    7 + 7 = 14.
  - Know the doubles of 1 to 20 by heart, and the doubles of tens (double 30 is
    60), of 25 (50), of 50 (100) and of 100 (200).
  - Double a 2-digit number by doubling the tens and the ones: double 34 is
    60 + 8 = 68.
  - HALF means splitting into 2 EQUAL parts. Half of 14 is 7 because 7 + 7 = 14.
    Halving undoes doubling.
  - Find half of every even number to 40, and half of tens: half of 60 is 30,
    half of 50 is 25, half of 100 is 50. An odd number cannot be split into two
    equal whole numbers.
  - Adding 0 or taking away 0 changes nothing: 9 + 0 = 9, 9 − 0 = 9. A number
    take away itself is 0: 9 − 9 = 0.
  - Multiplying as groups: 1 × 6 is 1 group of 6, which is 6; 6 × 1 is 6 groups
    of 1, which is 6. 0 × 6 (no groups) and 6 × 0 (6 empty groups) are both 0.
    Sharing 6 among 1 person: 6 ÷ 1 = 6.
  - Do not mix them up: 5 + 1 = 6 but 5 × 1 = 5; 5 + 0 = 5 but 5 × 0 = 0.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Riya wears 6 bangles on each hand. How many bangles does she wear?
    Answer: 12 bangles. Double 6 is 12: 6 + 6 = 12.
    Teacher explains: The same number twice is a DOUBLE. Show 6 on each hand (or
    6 counters in each of two rows). Say it together: "Double 6 is 12." Doubles
    are worth learning by heart because they help with so many other sums. Now
    ask: "What is double 7?"

D2. Ravi had 9 marbles. His uncle gave him the same number of marbles again. How
    many marbles does Ravi have now?
    Answer: 18 marbles. Double 9 is 18: 9 + 9 = 18.
    Teacher explains: "The same number again" means double. A trick for double
    9: double 10 is 20, and we have 1 less in each group, so 2 less: 18. Let the
    child check on a bead string: 9 and 9 more.

D3. Two friends each have ₹35. How much money do they have together?
    Answer: ₹70. Double 35 is 70.
    Teacher explains: Split 35 into tens and ones: 30 and 5. Double 30 is 60 (3
    tens and 3 tens are 6 tens). Double 5 is 10. Now 60 + 10 = 70. Show with ₹10
    notes and ₹5 coins: 3 notes and 1 coin for each friend. Then ask the child
    to double 24 in the same way (48).

D4. Nani shares 16 laddoos equally between 2 children. How many laddoos does
    each child get?
    Answer: 8 laddoos each. Half of 16 is 8.
    Teacher explains: Deal the laddoos out one by one: "one for you, one for
    you" until none are left, then count one pile: 8. Check with a double:
    8 + 8 = 16. Halving and doubling are opposites. Ask: "What is half of 18?
    How do you know?" (9, because 9 + 9 = 18.)

D5. Amma gives ₹30 to share equally between Anu and Meena. How much does each
    girl get?
    Answer: ₹15 each. Half of 30 is 15.
    Teacher explains: Split 30 into 20 and 10. Half of 20 is 10, half of 10 is
    5, and 10 + 5 = 15. Check: 15 + 15 = 30. Use three ₹10 notes: each girl gets
    one note, and the last note is changed into two ₹5 coins. Now try half of
    50.

D6. Can 9 mangoes be shared equally between 2 children without cutting any
    mango?
    Answer: No. Each child gets 4 mangoes and 1 mango is left over. (If we cut
    the last mango, each child gets 4 and a half.)
    Teacher explains: Deal the mangoes out in pairs; one is left without a
    partner. 9 is an ODD number, so it has no whole-number half. Even numbers
    (2, 4, 6, 8, 10 ...) can be halved exactly. Ask the child to find which
    numbers from 10 to 20 can be halved exactly.

D7. Sonu had scored 23 runs. In the next over he scored 0 runs. What is his
    score now?
    Answer: Still 23 runs. 23 + 0 = 23.
    Teacher explains: Adding 0 means adding nothing, so the number stays the
    same. The same is true for taking away 0: 23 − 0 = 23. Ask quick questions:
    "47 + 0? 0 + 85? 60 − 0?"

D8. 14 birds sit on a wire. All 14 fly away. How many birds are left?
    Answer: 0 birds. 14 − 14 = 0.
    Teacher explains: Any number take away itself is 0: nothing is left. Compare
    the two zero rules side by side: 14 − 0 = 14 (take away nothing) and
    14 − 14 = 0 (take away everything). Children mix these up, so say both aloud
    together.

D9. One plate has 7 idlis. How many idlis is that? Now 7 plates each have 1
    idli. How many idlis is that?
    Answer: 7 idlis both times. 1 × 7 = 7 and 7 × 1 = 7.
    Teacher explains: × means "groups of". 1 × 7 is 1 group of 7: 7. 7 × 1 is 7
    groups of 1: also 7. Multiplying by 1 leaves the number the same. The same
    with sharing: 7 idlis shared with only 1 person, he gets all 7, so
    7 ÷ 1 = 7. Let the child set out plates and idlis (or counters) and try
    1 × 5 and 5 × 1.

D10. There are 4 lunch boxes, but they are all empty. How many sandwiches are
     there? And if there are 0 boxes, with 5 sandwiches in each, how many
     sandwiches?
     Answer: 0 sandwiches both times. 4 × 0 = 0 and 0 × 5 = 0.
     Teacher explains: 4 groups of nothing is nothing. No groups at all is also
     nothing. So any number times 0 is 0, and 0 times any number is 0. Show 4
     empty cups and ask how many sweets are in them.

D11. Anu wrote 8 × 1 = 9 and 8 × 0 = 8. Help her put it right.
     Answer: 8 × 1 = 8 and 8 × 0 = 0.
     Teacher explains: Anu used the ADDING rules: 8 + 1 = 9 and 8 + 0 = 8. Ask
     her to read × as "groups of": 8 groups of 1 is 8; 8 groups of 0 is 0. Make
     a little table together: 8 + 1 = 9, 8 × 1 = 8, 8 + 0 = 8, 8 × 0 = 0. Praise
     her for knowing the adding rules so well!

D12. A money plant has 3 leaves. Each week the number of leaves doubles. How
     many leaves after 1, 2, 3 and 4 weeks? Then count back by halving.
     Answer: 6, 12, 24, 48 leaves. Halving back: 48, 24, 12, 6, 3.
     Teacher explains: Double 3 is 6, double 6 is 12, double 12 is 24 (20 + 4),
     double 24 is 48 (40 + 8). To go back, halve: half of 48 is 24, half of 24
     is 12, and so on. Write the chain with arrows both ways. Can the child
     start with 5? (10, 20, 40, 80.)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Doubles of 1 to 20. Add.
    a) 1 + 1 = __     b) 2 + 2 = __     c) 3 + 3 = __     d) 4 + 4 = __
    e) 5 + 5 = __     f) 6 + 6 = __     g) 7 + 7 = __     h) 8 + 8 = __
    i) 9 + 9 = __     j) 10 + 10 = __   k) 11 + 11 = __   l) 12 + 12 = __
    m) 13 + 13 = __   n) 14 + 14 = __   o) 15 + 15 = __   p) 16 + 16 = __
    q) 17 + 17 = __   r) 18 + 18 = __   s) 19 + 19 = __   t) 20 + 20 = __
    Answers: a) 2   b) 4   c) 6   d) 8   e) 10   f) 12   g) 14   h) 16   i) 18
             j) 20   k) 22   l) 24   m) 26   n) 28   o) 30   p) 32   q) 34
             r) 36   s) 38   t) 40

M2. Double the tens (and some special numbers).
    a) double 10    b) double 20    c) double 30    d) double 40
    e) double 50    f) double 60    g) double 70    h) double 80
    i) double 90    j) double 100   k) double 15    l) double 25
    m) double 35    n) double 45    o) double 200   p) double 500
    Answers: a) 20   b) 40   c) 60   d) 80   e) 100   f) 120   g) 140   h) 160
             i) 180   j) 200   k) 30   l) 50   m) 70   n) 90   o) 400   p) 1000

M3. Double each number: double the tens, double the ones, then add.
    a) double 12   b) double 13   c) double 14   d) double 21   e) double 23
    f) double 24   g) double 31   h) double 32   i) double 34   j) double 41
    k) double 42   l) double 43   m) double 16   n) double 17   o) double 18
    p) double 26   q) double 27   r) double 28   s) double 36   t) double 38
    Answers:
      a) 20 + 4 = 24
      b) 20 + 6 = 26
      c) 20 + 8 = 28
      d) 40 + 2 = 42
      e) 40 + 6 = 46
      f) 40 + 8 = 48
      g) 60 + 2 = 62
      h) 60 + 4 = 64
      i) 60 + 8 = 68
      j) 80 + 2 = 82
      k) 80 + 4 = 84
      l) 80 + 6 = 86
      m) 20 + 12 = 32
      n) 20 + 14 = 34
      o) 20 + 16 = 36
      p) 40 + 12 = 52
      q) 40 + 14 = 54
      r) 40 + 16 = 56
      s) 60 + 12 = 72
      t) 60 + 16 = 76

M4. Halves of even numbers to 20. Check with a double.
    a) half of 2    b) half of 4    c) half of 6    d) half of 8
    e) half of 10   f) half of 12   g) half of 14   h) half of 16
    i) half of 18   j) half of 20
    Answers: a) 1 (1 + 1 = 2)   b) 2 (2 + 2 = 4)   c) 3 (3 + 3 = 6)
             d) 4 (4 + 4 = 8)   e) 5 (5 + 5 = 10)   f) 6 (6 + 6 = 12)
             g) 7 (7 + 7 = 14)   h) 8 (8 + 8 = 16)   i) 9 (9 + 9 = 18)
             j) 10 (10 + 10 = 20)

M5. Halves of bigger even numbers: halve the tens, halve the ones, then add.
    (For 34: half of 30 is 15, half of 4 is 2.)
    a) half of 22   b) half of 24   c) half of 26   d) half of 28
    e) half of 30   f) half of 32   g) half of 34   h) half of 36
    i) half of 38   j) half of 40   k) half of 44   l) half of 46
    m) half of 48   n) half of 62   o) half of 64   p) half of 86
    Answers:
      a) 10 + 1 = 11
      b) 10 + 2 = 12
      c) 10 + 3 = 13
      d) 10 + 4 = 14
      e) 15
      f) 15 + 1 = 16
      g) 15 + 2 = 17
      h) 15 + 3 = 18
      i) 15 + 4 = 19
      j) 20
      k) 20 + 2 = 22
      l) 20 + 3 = 23
      m) 20 + 4 = 24
      n) 30 + 1 = 31
      o) 30 + 2 = 32
      p) 40 + 3 = 43

M6. Halves of tens and hundreds.
    a) half of 10     b) half of 20     c) half of 30     d) half of 40
    e) half of 50     f) half of 60     g) half of 70     h) half of 80
    i) half of 90     j) half of 100    k) half of 200    l) half of 120
    m) half of 140    n) half of 160    o) half of 180    p) half of 1000
    Answers: a) 5   b) 10   c) 15   d) 20   e) 25   f) 30   g) 35   h) 40
             i) 45   j) 50   k) 100   l) 60   m) 70   n) 80   o) 90   p) 500

M7. Doubling and halving are opposites. Fill in both blanks with numbers.
    a) double 8 = __, half of __ = 8       b) double 12 = __, half of __ = 12
    c) double 15 = __, half of __ = 15     d) double 20 = __, half of __ = 20
    e) double 7 = __, half of __ = 7       f) double 25 = __, half of __ = 25
    g) double 11 = __, half of __ = 11     h) double 30 = __, half of __ = 30
    i) double 19 = __, half of __ = 19     j) double 50 = __, half of __ = 50
    k) double 45 = __, half of __ = 45     l) double 100 = __, half of __ = 100
    Answers: a) 16, 16   b) 24, 24   c) 30, 30   d) 40, 40   e) 14, 14
             f) 50, 50   g) 22, 22   h) 60, 60   i) 38, 38   j) 100, 100
             k) 90, 90   l) 200, 200

M8. Adding 0, taking away 0, and taking away everything.
    a) 9 + 0 = __       b) 0 + 9 = __       c) 9 − 0 = __
    d) 9 − 9 = __       e) 15 + 0 = __      f) 0 + 38 = __
    g) 38 − 0 = __      h) 38 − 38 = __     i) 100 + 0 = __
    j) 0 + 0 = __       k) 100 − 100 = __   l) 64 − 0 = __
    m) 0 + 250 = __     n) 250 − 250 = __   o) 1 − 0 = __
    p) 1 − 1 = __       q) __ + 0 = 47      r) 73 − __ = 73
    s) 56 − __ = 0      t) __ − 0 = 0
    Answers: a) 9   b) 9   c) 9   d) 0   e) 15   f) 38   g) 38   h) 0   i) 100
             j) 0   k) 0   l) 64   m) 250   n) 0   o) 1   p) 0   q) 47   r) 0
             s) 56   t) 0

M9. Multiplying by 1 (1 group, or groups of 1). Find the answer.
    a) 4 × 1         b) 1 × 9         c) 1 × 1         d) 1 × 12
    e) 0 × 1         f) 1 × 20        g) 7 × 1         h) 1 × 35
    i) 3 × 1         j) 1 × 50        k) 16 × 1        l) 1 × 100
    m) __ × 1 = 8    n) 1 × __ = 25   o) __ × 1 = 1    p) 1 × 1 = __
    Answers: a) 4   b) 9   c) 1   d) 12   e) 0   f) 20   g) 7   h) 35   i) 3
             j) 50   k) 16   l) 100   m) 8   n) 25   o) 1   p) 1

M10. Multiplying by 0 (no groups, or empty groups). Find the answer.
     a) 5 × 0         b) 0 × 9         c) 1 × 0         d) 0 × 14
     e) 30 × 0        f) 0 × 0         g) 8 × 0         h) 0 × 100
     i) 2 × 0         j) 0 × 47        k) 6 × 0         l) 0 × 75
     m) 3 × 0 = __    n) 0 × 60 = __   o) 20 × __ = 0   p) __ × 5 = 0
     Answers: a) 0   b) 0   c) 0   d) 0   e) 0   f) 0   g) 0   h) 0   i) 0
              j) 0   k) 0   l) 0   m) 0   n) 0   o) 0   p) 0

M11. Careful! +, −, × and ÷ with 0 and 1. Find each answer.
     a) 6 + 1     b) 6 × 1     c) 6 − 1     d) 6 ÷ 1     e) 6 + 0     f) 6 × 0
     g) 6 − 0     h) 12 ÷ 1    i) 12 × 1    j) 12 + 1    k) 12 × 0    l) 12 − 12
     m) 1 × 1     n) 1 + 1     o) 0 ÷ 1     p) 0 + 1     q) 40 ÷ 1    r) 40 × 0
     s) 40 + 0    t) 40 − 1
     Answers: a) 7   b) 6   c) 5   d) 6   e) 6   f) 0   g) 6   h) 12   i) 12
              j) 13   k) 0   l) 0   m) 1   n) 2   o) 0   p) 1   q) 40   r) 0
              s) 40   t) 39

M12. Mixed. Fill in the blank.
     a) 9 × __ = 9                   b) __ × 7 = 0
     c) 15 − __ = 15                 d) 15 − __ = 0
     e) __ + 0 = 36                  f) double __ = 18
     g) half of __ = 9               h) double 13 = __
     i) half of 26 = __              j) __ ÷ 1 = 11
     k) 1 × __ = 90                  l) double 50 = __
     m) half of __ = 50              n) 40 = double __
     o) __ + __ = 30 (same number)   p) half of 36 = __
     Answers: a) 1   b) 0   c) 0   d) 15   e) 36   f) 9   g) 18   h) 26   i) 13
              j) 11   k) 90   l) 100   m) 100   n) 20   o) 15   p) 18

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: double with a mirror (put 4 counters in front of a mirror and
  count what you see), halve by dealing out real objects, and act out the 0 and
  1 rules with plates and counters; learn doubles to 10 before doubles to 20.
  Harder: double and halve 2- and 3-digit numbers (double 145, half of 90),
  build doubling chains (1, 2, 4, 8 ... 1024), and ask "Which number is its own
  double?" (0).


================================================================================
TOPIC 3.12 — TWO-DIGIT ADDITION AND SUBTRACTION WITHOUT REGROUPING
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - Add and take away TENS: 30 + 40 = 70 (3 tens and 4 tens are 7 tens),
    70 − 20 = 50 (7 tens take away 2 tens is 5 tens).
  - Add or take away a 1-digit number when the ones do not go past 10 or below
    0: 43 + 5 = 48, 67 − 4 = 63. The tens stay the same.
  - Add or take away tens to a 2-digit number: 36 + 20 = 56, 58 − 30 = 28. Only
    the tens digit changes.
  - Add and take away two 2-digit numbers with no regrouping: ones with ones,
    tens with tens. 34 + 25 = 59 and 78 − 43 = 35.
  - Write sums in COLUMNS: ones under ones, tens under tens. Always start with
    the ONES column.
  - Show why it works with bundles of 10 sticks and loose sticks, or with ₹10
    notes and ₹1 coins.
  - Check a take-away sum by adding: 78 − 43 = 35, and 35 + 43 = 78.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Ravi has 3 bundles of 10 sticks. Riya has 4 bundles of 10 sticks. How many
    sticks do they have together?
    Answer: 70 sticks. 30 + 40 = 70.
    Teacher explains: Count the bundles, not the sticks: 3 bundles and 4 bundles
    are 7 bundles, and 7 bundles of ten are 70. So 30 + 40 is as easy as
    3 + 4 = 7, with the word "tens" after it. Ask the child to say it in tens:
    "3 tens plus 4 tens is 7 tens, which is 70." Now try 50 + 20.

D2. A shop had 70 notebooks in 7 packets of 10. It sold 2 packets. How many
    notebooks are left?
    Answer: 50 notebooks. 70 − 20 = 50.
    Teacher explains: 7 tens take away 2 tens leaves 5 tens: 50. It works just
    like 7 − 2 = 5. Use real packets or bundles and let the child take away 2
    bundles. Then try 90 − 60.

D3. Anu had 43 stickers. Her friend gave her 5 more. How many stickers does Anu
    have now?
    Answer: 48 stickers. 43 + 5 = 48.
    Teacher explains: 43 is 4 tens and 3 ones. We add 5 more ONES: 3 + 5 = 8
    ones. The 4 tens do not change. So 43 + 5 = 48. Show with 4 bundles and 3
    loose sticks; add 5 loose sticks. Count on from 43 to check: 44, 45, 46, 47,
    48.

D4. The bus conductor had 67 tickets. He sold 4 tickets. How many tickets are
    left?
    Answer: 63 tickets. 67 − 4 = 63.
    Teacher explains: Take away from the ONES: 7 − 4 = 3. The 6 tens stay: 63.
    Ask: "Which digit changed? Which stayed the same?" Count back to check: 66,
    65, 64, 63.

D5. A basket has 36 mangoes. Dadaji puts in 20 more. How many mangoes are in the
    basket?
    Answer: 56 mangoes. 36 + 20 = 56.
    Teacher explains: 20 is 2 tens and 0 ones, so only the tens change: 3 tens +
    2 tens = 5 tens, and the 6 ones stay. On a hundred chart, adding 10 is one
    jump DOWN, so adding 20 is two jumps down: 36, 46, 56.

D6. The school library has 34 story books and 25 picture books. How many books
    is that? Work it out in columns.
          T O
          3 4
        + 2 5
        -----
          5 9
    Answer: 59 books. 34 + 25 = 59.
    Teacher explains: Write the numbers one under the other: ones under ones (4
    and 5), tens under tens (3 and 2). Step 1: add the ONES: 4 + 5 = 9; write 9
    in the ones column. Step 2: add the TENS: 3 + 2 = 5; write 5 in the tens
    column. The answer is 59. Now you do 42 + 36 in columns.

D7. 78 children are in the hall. 43 go out for games. How many children are
    still in the hall? Work it out in columns.
          T O
          7 8
        − 4 3
        -----
          3 5
    Answer: 35 children. 78 − 43 = 35.
    Teacher explains: Ones first: 8 − 3 = 5. Then tens: 7 − 4 = 3. The answer is
    35. Always put the BIGGER number on top when you take away. Show it with 7
    bundles and 8 sticks: take away 4 bundles and 3 sticks. Now try 96 − 51.

D8. Nani has ₹52. Nana gives her ₹36. How much money does Nani have now?
    Answer: ₹88. 52 + 36 = 88.
    Teacher explains: Use ₹10 notes and ₹1 coins. ₹52 is 5 notes and 2 coins;
    ₹36 is 3 notes and 6 coins. Notes: 5 + 3 = 8 notes (₹80). Coins: 2 + 6 = 8
    coins (₹8). Together ₹88. This is the same as adding tens with tens and ones
    with ones.

D9. Sonu worked out 42 + 5 like this and got 92. What went wrong?
          4 2
        + 5
        -----
          9 2
    Answer: He put the 5 under the tens. 5 is 5 ones, so it goes under the 2.
    The right answer is 47.
    Teacher explains: Line up the ONES first, at the right-hand side. 42 has 2
    ones and 5 has 5 ones, so 2 + 5 = 7 ones, and the 4 tens stay: 47. A quick
    sense check helps: adding 5 to 42 cannot make 92! Write "T" and "O" at the
    top of the columns until the child always lines up correctly.

D10. Meena worked out 89 − 56 = 33. How can she check her answer?
     Answer: Add the answer to the number taken away: 33 + 56 = 89. It matches,
     so she is right.
     Teacher explains: Taking away and adding are opposites (they are one fact
     family). If 89 − 56 = 33, then 33 + 56 must be 89. Add in columns: ones
     3 + 6 = 9, tens 3 + 5 = 8. Checking like this catches many mistakes. Let
     the child check 64 − 21 = 43 the same way.

D11. Our team scored 45 runs in the first innings and 32 runs in the second
     innings. How many runs is that in all? How many more runs did we score in
     the first innings?
     Answer: 77 runs in all (45 + 32 = 77). 13 more in the first innings
     (45 − 32 = 13).
     Teacher explains: Two questions, two sums. "In all" means add: ones
     5 + 2 = 7, tens 4 + 3 = 7, so 77. "How many more" means find the
     difference: ones 5 − 2 = 3, tens 4 − 3 = 1, so 13. Draw two bars, one for
     each innings, to show the difference.

D12. Some digits are hidden: 4_ + _3 = 68. Find the hidden digits.
     Answer: 45 + 23 = 68.
     Teacher explains: Work column by column. Ones: something + 3 = 8, so the
     missing ones digit is 5. Tens: 4 + something = 6, so the missing tens digit
     is 2. Check: 45 + 23 = 68. Puzzles like this show that the child really
     understands the columns. Praise good thinking!

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Add the tens.
    a) 10 + 20   b) 30 + 40   c) 50 + 20   d) 60 + 30   e) 20 + 20   f) 40 + 50
    g) 70 + 10   h) 10 + 80   i) 30 + 30   j) 20 + 60   k) 40 + 40   l) 50 + 30
    m) 60 + 20   n) 10 + 50   o) 30 + 60   p) 50 + 50   q) 70 + 20   r) 20 + 70
    s) 80 + 10   t) 40 + 60
    Answers: a) 30   b) 70   c) 70   d) 90   e) 40   f) 90   g) 80   h) 90
             i) 60   j) 80   k) 80   l) 80   m) 80   n) 60   o) 90   p) 100
             q) 90   r) 90   s) 90   t) 100

M2. Take away the tens.
    a) 70 − 20    b) 50 − 30    c) 90 − 40    d) 80 − 50    e) 60 − 10
    f) 40 − 20    g) 90 − 70    h) 30 − 10    i) 80 − 20    j) 100 − 40
    k) 70 − 50    l) 60 − 60    m) 50 − 10    n) 90 − 30    o) 80 − 80
    p) 100 − 90   q) 40 − 30    r) 70 − 40    s) 60 − 20    t) 90 − 50
    Answers: a) 50   b) 20   c) 50   d) 30   e) 50   f) 20   g) 20   h) 20
             i) 60   j) 60   k) 20   l) 0   m) 40   n) 60   o) 0   p) 10   q) 10
             r) 30   s) 40   t) 40

M3. Add the ones. (The tens stay the same.)
    a) 43 + 5   b) 21 + 7   c) 62 + 4   d) 35 + 3   e) 54 + 2   f) 71 + 8

M4. Take away the ones. (The tens stay the same.)
    a) 67 − 4   b) 29 − 5   c) 48 − 3   d) 86 − 6   e) 35 − 2   f) 59 − 7

M6. Add. Ones first, then tens. (Write them in columns if it helps.)
    a) 34 + 25   b) 42 + 36   c) 51 + 27   d) 63 + 24   e) 15 + 42   f) 71 + 18

M12. Find the hidden digits (each _ is one digit).
     a) 4_ + _3 = 68   b) _2 + 3_ = 57   c) _5 + 4_ = 99   d) _8 − 2_ = 51
     e) 3_ + _4 = 79   f) 9_ − _3 = 65   g) _1 + 5_ = 88   h) _6 − 4_ = 23
     i) 2_ + _6 = 59   j) 8_ − _2 = 35
     Answers: a) 45 + 23 = 68   b) 22 + 35 = 57   c) 55 + 44 = 99
              d) 78 − 27 = 51   e) 35 + 44 = 79   f) 98 − 33 = 65
              g) 31 + 57 = 88   h) 66 − 43 = 23   i) 23 + 36 = 59
              j) 87 − 52 = 35

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: build both numbers with bundles and loose sticks (or ₹10 notes and
  ₹1 coins) before writing anything; use squared paper with T and O at the top
  of the columns; do tens-only and ones-only sums first. Harder: do the sums in
  your head without columns, add three 2-digit numbers (21 + 32 + 45), try
  3-digit sums with no regrouping (324 + 152), and make up hidden-digit puzzles.


================================================================================
TOPIC 3.13 — TWO- AND THREE-DIGIT ADDITION AND SUBTRACTION WITH REGROUPING
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - When the ONES add up to 10 or more, make a ten and CARRY it to the tens
    column (10 ones = 1 ten). 47 + 25: 7 + 5 = 12, write 2, carry 1.
  - When the TENS add up to 10 or more, carry 1 hundred to the hundreds column
    (10 tens = 1 hundred).
  - When there are not enough ones to take away, REGROUP (borrow): change 1 ten
    into 10 ones. 52 − 27: 2 ones is too few, so 5 tens 2 ones becomes 4 tens 12
    ones. Do the same with hundreds and tens.
  - Always start with the ONES column and move left.
  - Zeros in the middle: in 402 − 175 there are no tens to regroup, so first
    change 1 hundred into 10 tens, then 1 ten into 10 ones: 402 is 3 hundreds, 9
    tens and 12 ones.
  - Check a take-away sum by adding the answer to the number taken away.
  - Before you start, estimate (see 3.15) so you know roughly what the answer
    should be.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Class 2A has 47 children and class 2B has 25 children. How many children are
    there in the two classes?
          T O
          1
          4 7
        + 2 5
        -----
          7 2
    Answer: 72 children. 47 + 25 = 72.
    Teacher explains: Ones first: 7 + 5 = 12. 12 ones is 1 ten and 2 ones, so
    write 2 in the ones column and carry the 1 ten to the top of the tens column
    (the small 1). Tens: 4 + 2 = 6, and the carried 1 makes 7. Answer 72. Show
    it with sticks: 12 loose sticks are too many, so tie 10 of them into a
    bundle and move it to the tens. Now you try 36 + 48.

D2. A fruit seller has 38 mangoes in one basket and 46 in another. How many
    mangoes does he have?
          T O
          1
          3 8
        + 4 6
        -----
          8 4
    Answer: 84 mangoes. 38 + 46 = 84.
    Teacher explains: Ones: 8 + 6 = 14. Write 4, carry 1 ten. Tens:
    3 + 4 + 1 = 8. Answer 84. A common mistake is to write 714 (the whole 14 in
    the ones place). Remind the child: only ONE digit fits in each column.
    Estimate to check: 40 + 50 = 90, so 84 is sensible.

D3. Ravi has ₹52. He spends ₹27 on a kite. How much money is left?
           T  O
           4 12
           5  2
        −  2  7
        -------
           2  5
    Answer: ₹25. 52 − 27 = 25.
    Teacher explains: Ones: 2 − 7? We cannot take 7 from 2. So we regroup: take
    1 ten from the 5 tens (leaving 4 tens) and change it into 10 ones:
    2 + 10 = 12 ones. The small 4 and 12 on top show this. Now ones: 12 − 7 = 5.
    Tens: 4 − 2 = 2. Answer 25. With money: change one ₹10 note into ten ₹1
    coins. Check: 25 + 27 = 52.

D4. Amma made 60 idlis. The family ate 24. How many idlis are left?
           T  O
           5 10
           6  0
        −  2  4
        -------
           3  6
    Answer: 36 idlis. 60 − 24 = 36.
    Teacher explains: Ones: 0 − 4? No ones at all! Regroup 1 ten: 6 tens becomes
    5 tens and 0 ones becomes 10 ones. Ones: 10 − 4 = 6. Tens: 5 − 2 = 3. Answer
    36. Check: 36 + 24 = 60. Now try 80 − 35.

D5. A school has 256 boys and 138 girls. How many children are in the school?
          H T O
            1
          2 5 6
        + 1 3 8
        -------
          3 9 4
    Answer: 394 children. 256 + 138 = 394.
    Teacher explains: Three columns now: hundreds, tens, ones. Ones: 6 + 8 = 14,
    write 4, carry 1. Tens: 5 + 3 + 1 = 9. Hundreds: 2 + 1 = 3. Answer 394. Only
    one regrouping here. Use base-ten blocks: flats for hundreds, rods for tens,
    small cubes for ones.

D6. A village planted 375 trees last year and 248 trees this year. How many
    trees did they plant in the two years?
          H T O
          1 1
          3 7 5
        + 2 4 8
        -------
          6 2 3
    Answer: 623 trees. 375 + 248 = 623.
    Teacher explains: Two regroupings. Ones: 5 + 8 = 13, write 3, carry 1 ten.
    Tens: 7 + 4 + 1 = 12 tens, which is 1 hundred and 2 tens: write 2, carry 1
    hundred. Hundreds: 3 + 2 + 1 = 6. Answer 623. Estimate: 400 + 200 = 600,
    close to 623. Well done for keeping track of both carries!

D7. A library has 463 books. 128 books are lent out. How many books are still in
    the library?
           H  T  O
              5 13
           4  6  3
        −  1  2  8
        ----------
           3  3  5
    Answer: 335 books. 463 − 128 = 335.
    Teacher explains: Ones: 3 − 8? Regroup 1 ten: 6 tens become 5 tens, 3 ones
    become 13 ones. 13 − 8 = 5. Tens: 5 − 2 = 3. Hundreds: 4 − 1 = 3. Answer
    335. Check: 335 + 128 = 463.

D8. A stadium has 532 seats. 267 people have come. How many seats are empty?
           H  T  O
           4 12 12
           5  3  2
        −  2  6  7
        ----------
           2  6  5
    Answer: 265 seats. 532 − 267 = 265.
    Teacher explains: Ones: 2 − 7? Regroup a ten: 3 tens become 2 tens, 2 ones
    become 12 ones; 12 − 7 = 5. Tens: 2 − 6? Regroup a hundred: 5 hundreds
    become 4 hundreds, 2 tens become 12 tens; 12 − 6 = 6. Hundreds: 4 − 2 = 2.
    Answer 265. Go slowly, one column at a time, and cross out the old digits
    neatly.

D9. A shopkeeper had 402 eggs. He sold 175. How many eggs are left?
           H  T  O
           3  9 12
           4  0  2
        −  1  7  5
        ----------
           2  2  7
    Answer: 227 eggs. 402 − 175 = 227.
    Teacher explains: Ones: 2 − 5? We need a ten, but there are 0 tens! So go to
    the hundreds: change 1 hundred into 10 tens (4 hundreds become 3, 0 tens
    become 10). Now change 1 of those tens into 10 ones (10 tens become 9, 2
    ones become 12). So 402 is 3 hundreds, 9 tens and 12 ones. Now: 12 − 5 = 7,
    9 − 7 = 2, 3 − 1 = 2. Answer 227. Show it with ₹100 notes, ₹10 notes and ₹1
    coins at a pretend shop.

D10. How can we be sure that 402 − 175 = 227 is right?
     Answer: Add: 227 + 175 = 402. We get back the number we started with, so
     the answer is right.
     Teacher explains: Adding undoes taking away. Add in columns: ones
     7 + 5 = 12, write 2 carry 1; tens 2 + 7 + 1 = 10, write 0 carry 1; hundreds
     2 + 1 + 1 = 4. We get 402. Make checking a habit after every take-away sum.
     Now check 532 − 267 = 265 yourself.

D11. Sonu worked out 52 − 27 and got 35. What did he do wrong?
     Answer: He took the smaller digit from the bigger one in the ones
     (7 − 2 = 5) instead of regrouping. The right answer is 25.
     Teacher explains: This is the most common mistake. In the ones column the
     TOP number is 2 and we must take 7 away from it. We cannot turn it round!
     Regroup: 4 tens and 12 ones, 12 − 7 = 5, 4 − 2 = 2. Checking by adding
     shows the mistake: 35 + 27 = 62, not 52. Praise Sonu for working neatly,
     and practise a few more together.

D12. Papa has ₹300. He buys a school bag for ₹146. How much money is left?
            H  T  O
            2  9 10
            3  0  0
         −  1  4  6
         ----------
            1  5  4
     Answer: ₹154. 300 − 146 = 154.
     Teacher explains: Two zeros! Change 1 hundred into 10 tens (3 hundreds
     become 2, 0 tens become 10), then 1 ten into 10 ones (10 tens become 9, 0
     ones become 10). So 300 is 2 hundreds, 9 tens and 10 ones. Ones:
     10 − 6 = 4. Tens: 9 − 4 = 5. Hundreds: 2 − 1 = 1. Answer ₹154. Check:
     154 + 146 = 300. Now try 500 − 238.

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------

M5. Take away 2-digit numbers. Regroup a ten.
    a) 52 − 27   b) 60 − 24   c) 83 − 47   d) 71 − 38   e) 45 − 19   f) 90 − 56
