       g) 10, 19, 28, 37, 46, 55, 64, 73, 82, 91

M11. Even or odd? For the sums, work out the answer and say if it is even or
     odd.
     a) 14        b) 27        c) 30        d) 45        e) 58
     f) 61        g) 99        h) 100       i) 236       j) 403
     k) 4 + 6     l) 3 + 5     m) 8 + 7     n) 11 + 13   o) 20 + 9
     p) 15 + 16
     Answers: a) even   b) odd   c) even   d) odd   e) even   f) odd   g) odd
              h) even   i) even   j) odd   k) 10, even   l) 8, even   m) 15, odd
              n) 24, even   o) 29, odd   p) 31, odd

M12. Growing and repeating patterns. Find the missing numbers.
     a) 1, 3, 5, 7, __, __
     b) 1, 4, 7, 10, __, __
     c) 1, 4, 9, 16, __, __
     d) 1, 3, 6, 10, __, __
     e) 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, __, __
     f) 3, 6, 12, 24, __
     g) 1, 2, 3, 1, 2, 3 ...: the 10th number
     h) 1, 2, 3, 1, 2, 3 ...: the 15th number
     i) 5, 0, 5, 0 ...: the 9th number
     j) 1, 2, 1, 2 ...: the 20th number
     k) 2, 4, 6, 2, 4, 6 ...: the 8th number
     l) 10, 20, 30, 10, 20, 30 ...: the 12th number
     Answers:
       a) 9, 11 (+2)
       b) 13, 16 (+3)
       c) 25, 36 (add 3, 5, 7, 9, 11 ...)
       d) 15, 21 (add 2, 3, 4, 5, 6 ...)
       e) 32, 64 (double)
       f) 48 (double)
       g) 1
       h) 3
       i) 5
       j) 2
       k) 4
       l) 30

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: use a number line on the floor and jump the pattern with your
  whole body; colour the pattern on a printed hundred chart (all +2 numbers
  red, all +5 numbers blue) and look for stripes and columns. Start with +10
  and +2 before +5, +3 and +4. Harder: make up a sequence for a partner to
  crack; find numbers that are in BOTH the +2 and the +5 sequences from 0 (10,
  20, 30 ... ); continue patterns past 1000; build growing patterns with
  matchsticks (1 square needs 4 sticks, 2 squares in a row 7, 3 squares 10 —
  rule +3).


================================================================================
TOPIC 3.30 — DATA: BAR GRAPHS AND PICTOGRAPHS, ASKING AND ANSWERING QUESTIONS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - Read a PICTOGRAPH where one picture stands for more than one thing: ● = 2,
    ● = 5 or ● = 10. Always read the KEY first. Half a picture (○ in this
    file) stands for half the key: 1 when ● = 2, 5 when ● = 10.
  - Read a BAR GRAPH: find the top of the bar, go across to the number scale
    and read the value. The scale may count in 1s, 2s or 5s (or 10s).
  - Answer questions from a graph: how many in a group, which is most and
    least, how many more or fewer, how many altogether.
  - Make a pictograph or a bar graph from a tally chart or table: choose a
    title, labels, a key or scale, and draw.
  - Ask good questions of data, and plan a small survey: a question, the
    choices, a tally, a graph, and what the graph tells us.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. A class voted for their favourite sport. Key: ● = 2 children, ○ = 1 child.
    How many children chose each sport? Which is the favourite? How many
    children voted?
        Cricket   : ● ● ● ● ● ●
        Football  : ● ● ● ●
        Kabaddi   : ● ● ○
        Badminton : ● ○
    Answer: Cricket 12, Football 8, Kabaddi 5, Badminton 3. Cricket is the
    favourite. 28 children voted.
    Teacher explains: Read the key first: each ● is 2 children, so count the ●
    in 2s: cricket 2, 4, 6, 8, 10, 12. Half a picture is half of 2, which is
    1: kabaddi is 2, 4 and 1 more = 5. For the total, add the numbers, not the
    pictures: 12 + 8 = 20, 20 + 5 = 25, 25 + 3 = 28. Now ask the child: "How
    many more chose cricket than kabaddi?" (7)

D2. On Diwali night, four families count the diyas they light. Key: ● = 5
    diyas. Which family lit the most? How many diyas did the Khan family and
    the Das family light together?
        Sharma : ● ● ● ●
        Khan   : ● ● ●
        Iyer   : ● ● ● ● ●
        Das    : ● ●
    Answer: The Iyer family lit the most (25). Khan and Das together:
    15 + 10 = 25.
    Teacher explains: Count in 5s along each row: Sharma 5, 10, 15, 20. Khan
    15, Iyer 25, Das 10. The longest row is the most, but we still count to
    know the number. Ask: "How many diyas in all?" (70)

D3. The school library shows how many books each class read in a month. Key:
    ● = 10 books, ○ = 5 books. How many did Class 2 read? How many more did
    Class 3 read than Class 1? How many in all?
        Class 1 : ● ● ●
        Class 2 : ● ● ● ● ○
        Class 3 : ● ● ● ● ●
        Class 4 : ● ● ○
    Answer: Class 2 read 45. Class 3 read 20 more than Class 1 (50 and 30). In
    all: 150 books.
    Teacher explains: Count the ● in 10s and add 5 for a ○: Class 2 is 10, 20,
    30, 40 and 5 = 45. For "how many more", find both numbers first, then
    count up from the smaller: 30 to 50 is 20. Total: 30 + 45 + 50 + 25; pair
    45 + 25 = 70 and 30 + 50 = 80, so 150.

D4. Children made a tally of vehicles passing the school: cars ||||/ ||||/
    ||||, buses ||||/ |, autos ||||/ ||||, bikes ||||/ ||||/ |. Make a
    pictograph with the key ● = 2 vehicles.
    Answer: Cars 14: 7 ●. Buses 6: 3 ●. Autos 9: 4 ● and ○. Bikes 11: 5 ● and
    ○.
    Teacher explains: Step 1: turn each tally into a number (count in 5s, then
    1s). Step 2: halve the number to find how many ● (each ● is 2). 14 is 7
    twos, so 7 pictures. 9 is 4 twos and 1 more, so 4 ● and half a picture.
    Step 3: draw the rows lined up on the left, and write the key and a title.
    Let the child do it on squared paper.

D5. This bar graph shows how many children were absent each day. On which day
    were the most absent? How many were absent in the whole week?
           5 |                               ■
           4 |                 ■             ■
           3 |   ■             ■             ■
           2 |   ■             ■      ■      ■
           1 |   ■      ■      ■      ■      ■
           0 +-----------------------------------
                Mon    Tue    Wed    Thu    Fri
    Answer: Most on Friday (5). In the whole week: 15.
    Teacher explains: Put a finger on the top of each bar and slide it across
    to the numbers on the left: Monday 3, Tuesday 1, Wednesday 4, Thursday 2,
    Friday 5. Here the scale goes up in 1s. Then add: 3 + 1 = 4, + 4 = 8, +
    2 = 10, + 5 = 15.

D6. This bar graph shows favourite fruits. The scale goes up in 2s. How many
    chose banana? How many more chose mango than grapes?
          10 |   ■
           8 |   ■             ■
           6 |   ■      ■      ■
           4 |   ■      ■      ■      ■
           2 |   ■      ■      ■      ■
           0 +----------------------------
               Mango  Apple  Banana Grapes
    Answer: Banana 8. Mango is 6 more than grapes (10 and 4).
    Teacher explains: Read the scale first: 2, 4, 6, 8, 10 — every line is 2
    more. The banana bar reaches the line marked 8. If a bar stopped halfway
    between 6 and 8, it would show 7. For "how many more", find both numbers
    and count up: 4 to 10 is 6.

D7. In a garden cricket match, the bar graph shows runs scored. The scale goes
    up in 5s. Who scored the most? How many more runs did Dia score than
    Chetan? How many runs in total?
          40 |                        ■
          35 |                        ■
          30 |   ■                    ■
          25 |   ■      ■             ■
          20 |   ■      ■             ■
          15 |   ■      ■      ■      ■
          10 |   ■      ■      ■      ■
           5 |   ■      ■      ■      ■
           0 +----------------------------
               Aarav   Bina  Chetan  Dia
    Answer: Dia scored the most (40). Dia scored 25 more than Chetan. Total:
    110 runs.
    Teacher explains: Count up the scale in 5s: 5, 10, 15 ... Dia's bar
    reaches 40, Chetan's reaches 15. Count up from 15 to 40: 5 to 20, then 20
    more to 40 = 25. Total: 30 + 25 + 15 + 40; 25 + 15 = 40, 40 + 40 = 80,
    80 + 30 = 110.

D8. The table shows the pets of children in Class 2: Dog 8, Cat 6, Fish 2,
    Parrot 4. How do we draw a bar graph of it?
           8 |   ■
           6 |   ■      ■
           4 |   ■      ■             ■
           2 |   ■      ■      ■      ■
           0 +----------------------------
                Dog    Cat    Fish  Parrot
    Answer: Draw an up-line with a scale 0, 2, 4, 6, 8 and a line along the
    bottom with the names Dog, Cat, Fish, Parrot. Draw bars up to 8, 6, 2 and
    4. Write the title "Pets in Class 2".
    Teacher explains: Choose the scale by looking at the biggest number (8) —
    counting in 2s keeps the graph short. Space the bars equally and make them
    the same width. Use a ruler for the lines. Then ask 3 questions about your
    graph and answer them.

D9. Look at the fruit bar graph in D6. Make up three questions that the graph
    can answer, and answer them.
    Answer: For example: "Which fruit is liked least?" (grapes); "How many
    children answered?" (28); "How many more chose banana than apple?" (2).
    Teacher explains: A graph is made to answer questions. Give sentence
    starters: "Which is the most ...", "How many more ...", "How many
    altogether ...", "Is it true that ...". Also ask a question the graph
    CANNOT answer ("Which fruit does Ravi like?") — the graph does not show
    names.

D10. Plan a survey: "What is our favourite snack?" What are the steps?
     Answer: 1) Write the question. 2) Choose 4 or 5 choices (samosa, idli,
     fruit, biscuits, other). 3) Ask everyone and make a tally. 4) Count the
     tallies. 5) Draw a pictograph or bar graph. 6) Say what it tells us
     ("Samosa is the favourite").
     Teacher explains: Let the child do a real survey of family or classmates.
     Each person answers only once — tick names off a list. Talk about why the
     question must be clear ("favourite" means only one choice). This is how
     real scientists and newspapers collect data.

D11. Class A made a pictograph with ● = 2 and drew 4 pictures for mango. Class
     B used ● = 5 and drew 3 pictures for mango. Which class has more children
     who like mango?
     Answer: Class B: 3 × 5 = 15. Class A: 4 × 2 = 8. So Class B has more,
     even though it has fewer pictures.
     Teacher explains: Always read the key before comparing. Count Class A in
     2s: 2, 4, 6, 8. Count Class B in 5s: 5, 10, 15. More pictures does not
     always mean more. When we compare two pictographs, it is best if they use
     the same key.

D12. A pictograph shows the trees planted by each class. Key: ● = 10 trees,
     ○ = 5 trees. Class 2 wants to plant 20 more trees. How many pictures must
     be added to its row? How many trees will Class 2 then have?
         Class 1 : ● ● ●
         Class 2 : ● ● ● ●
         Class 3 : ● ● ● ● ● ○
     Answer: 2 more ● (20 is two tens). Class 2 will have 60 trees, more than
     Class 3 (55).
     Teacher explains: Read first: Class 2 has 4 ● = 40. 20 more trees = 2
     more ●. New total 40 + 20 = 60. Ask: "How would you show 5 more trees?"
     (half a picture, ○)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Key: ● = 2, ○ = 1. What number does each row show?
    a) ● ●
    b) ● ● ●
    c) ● ○
    d) ● ● ● ● ●
    e) ● ● ● ○
    f) ● ● ● ● ● ●
    g) ○
    h) ● ● ● ● ○
    i) ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ●
    j) ● ● ● ● ● ○
    k) ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ●
    l) ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ○
    Answers: a) 4   b) 6   c) 3   d) 10   e) 7   f) 12   g) 1   h) 9   i) 16
             j) 11   k) 20   l) 15

M2. Key: ● = 5. What number does each row show?
    a) ● ●
    b) ● ● ● ● ●
    c) ●
    d) ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ●
    e) ● ● ●
    f) ● ● ● ● ● ● ●
    g) ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ●
    h) ● ● ● ●
    i) ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ●
    j) ● ● ● ● ● ●
    Answers: a) 10   b) 25   c) 5   d) 40   e) 15   f) 35   g) 50   h) 20
             i) 45   j) 30

M3. Key: ● = 10, ○ = 5. What number does each row show?
    a) ● ● ●
    b) ● ● ● ● ●
    c) ● ○
    d) ● ● ● ● ● ● ●
    e) ● ● ○
    f) ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ●
    g) ○
    h) ● ● ● ● ○
    i) ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ○
    j) ● ● ● ● ● ●
    k) ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ● ○
    l) ● ● ● ○
    Answers: a) 30   b) 50   c) 15   d) 70   e) 25   f) 100   g) 5   h) 45
             i) 85   j) 60   k) 95   l) 35

M4. How many pictures do you draw? (● = the key; use ○ for half a picture.)
    a) 8 with ● = 2     b) 13 with ● = 2    c) 5 with ● = 2
    d) 18 with ● = 2    e) 15 with ● = 5    f) 30 with ● = 5
    g) 45 with ● = 5    h) 40 with ● = 10   i) 25 with ● = 10
    j) 65 with ● = 10   k) 90 with ● = 10   l) 55 with ● = 10
    Answers: a) 4 ●   b) 6 ● and ○   c) 2 ● and ○   d) 9 ●   e) 3 ●   f) 6 ●
             g) 9 ●   h) 4 ●   i) 2 ● and ○   j) 6 ● and ○   k) 9 ●
             l) 5 ● and ○

M5. Key: ● = 10, ○ = 5.
    Red    : ● ● ● ● ○
    Blue   : ● ● ● ● ● ● ●
    Green  : ● ● ●
    Yellow : ● ● ● ● ● ○
    White  : ● ● ●
    a) How many chose Yellow?
    b) Which colour is the most?
    c) Which colours are the least?
    d) Blue − Green: how many more?
    e) Yellow − Red: how many more?
    f) Red and Green together?
    g) How many in all?
    h) Which colours have the same number?
    Answers: a) 55   b) Blue   c) Green, White   d) 40   e) 10   f) 75   g) 230
             h) Green and White (30 each)

M6. Read the bar graph (scale 1).
       7 |            ■
       6 |  ■         ■
       5 |  ■         ■         ■
       4 |  ■         ■    ■    ■
       3 |  ■         ■    ■    ■
       2 |  ■    ■    ■    ■    ■
       1 |  ■    ■    ■    ■    ■
       0 +-------------------------
            A    B    C    D    E
    a) Value of each bar   b) Most                c) Least
    d) C − B               e) A + D               f) Total
    Answers: a) A 6, B 2, C 7, D 4, E 5   b) C   c) B   d) 5   e) 10   f) 24

M7. Read the bar graph (scale 2).
      14 |            ■
      12 |  ■         ■
      10 |  ■         ■         ■
       8 |  ■    ■    ■         ■
       6 |  ■    ■    ■    ■    ■
       4 |  ■    ■    ■    ■    ■
       2 |  ■    ■    ■    ■    ■
       0 +-------------------------
            P    Q    R    S    T
    a) Value of each bar             b) Most
    c) Least                         d) R − S
    e) P − Q                         f) Q + S
    g) Total                         h) Which bar is 4 less than P?
    Answers: a) P 12, Q 8, R 14, S 6, T 10   b) R   c) S   d) 8   e) 4   f) 14
             g) 50   h) Q (8)

M8. Read the bar graph (scale 5).
      40 |       ■
      35 |       ■
      30 |       ■         ■
      25 |  ■    ■         ■
      20 |  ■    ■         ■
      15 |  ■    ■    ■    ■
      10 |  ■    ■    ■    ■
       5 |  ■    ■    ■    ■
       0 +--------------------
            W    X    Y    Z
    a) Value of each bar
    b) Most
    c) Least
    d) X − Y
    e) Z − W
    f) W + Y
    g) Total
    h) How many more to make Y equal to X?
    Answers: a) W 25, X 40, Y 15, Z 30   b) X   c) Y   d) 25   e) 5   f) 40
             g) 110   h) 25

M9. Turn the tally chart into numbers, then draw a bar graph with scale 2.
    A  ||||/ |||
    B  ||||/ ||||/ ||
    C  ||
    D  ||||/ |
    Answers: A 8, B 12, C 2, D 6. Graph:
               12 |       ■
               10 |       ■
                8 |  ■    ■
                6 |  ■    ■         ■
                4 |  ■    ■         ■
                2 |  ■    ■    ■    ■
                0 +--------------------
                     A    B    C    D

M10. The lines of a bar graph are numbered 1, 2, 3 ... from the bottom. What
     value does the bar show?
     a) scale 1, bar reaches line 6
     b) scale 2, bar reaches line 4
     c) scale 2, bar reaches line 7
     d) scale 5, bar reaches line 3
     e) scale 5, bar reaches line 8
     f) scale 10, bar reaches line 5
     g) scale 2, halfway between lines 3 and 4
     h) scale 10, halfway between lines 2 and 3
     i) scale 2, halfway between lines 0 and 1
     j) scale 10, halfway between lines 7 and 8
     k) value 12 with scale 2: which line?
     l) value 35 with scale 5: which line?
     Answers: a) 6   b) 8   c) 14   d) 15   e) 40   f) 50   g) 7   h) 25   i) 1
              j) 75   k) line 6   l) line 7

M11. Table: Tea 24, Milk 18, Juice 12, Water 30, Lassi 18. True or false?
     a) Water is the most.
     b) Juice is the least.
     c) Tea is 6 more than Milk.
     d) Milk and Lassi are the same.
     e) Water is double Juice.
     f) Tea and Juice together make 36.
     g) There are 100 in all.
     h) Water is 12 more than Milk.
     i) With ● = 6, Tea needs 4 pictures.
     j) With ● = 6, Juice needs 3 pictures.
     Answers: a) true   b) true   c) true   d) true   e) false (double 12 is 24)
              f) true   g) false (102)   h) true   i) true   j) false (2)

M12. Find the key: what is one ● worth?
     a) 4 ● show 20     b) 3 ● show 6      c) 5 ● show 50     d) 6 ● show 12
     e) 7 ● show 35     f) 2 ● show 20     g) 8 ● show 40     h) 9 ● show 18
     i) 10 ● show 100   j) 3 ● show 15
     Answers: a) 5   b) 2   c) 10   d) 2   e) 5   f) 10   g) 5   h) 2   i) 10
              j) 5

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: build pictographs with real objects first (one block = 2
  children), then with stickers, then drawn. For bar graphs use squared paper,
  one square = 1, before using scales of 2 and 5. Always read the title and
  the key or scale aloud before any question. Harder: collect real data (birds
  seen, rainy days in a month, runs in a match), choose the best scale
  yourself, and draw the same data as a pictograph AND a bar graph; write 5
  questions for a friend; spot mistakes in a graph with a wrong key or unequal
  bars.


================================================================================
TOPIC 3.31 — SIMPLE LOGICAL PUZZLES AND REASONING GAMES
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - Solve "Who am I?" number riddles to 100 by using one clue at a time to
    cross out numbers.
  - Complete magic squares (every row, column and diagonal has the same
    total), number pyramids (each brick is the sum of the two below it),
    missing-number grids and 4 × 4 sudoku.
  - Decide if a statement is ALWAYS true, SOMETIMES true or NEVER true, and
    give an example or a counter-example.
  - Use "if ... then ..." reasoning, and put things in order from clues
    (taller than, older than, ahead of).
  - Find the odd one out and explain the reason (more than one answer can be
    right if the reason is good).
  - Solve simple logic grids and symbol puzzles (▲ + ▲ = 10), and count all
    the possible ways in an organised list.
  - Explain your thinking aloud: "I know ... because ...".

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Who am I? "I am a 2-digit number. My tens digit is 4. My ones digit is 2
    more than my tens digit."
    Answer: 46.
    Teacher explains: Use one clue at a time. Clue 1: 2-digit, so 10 to 99.
    Clue 2: tens digit 4, so 40 to 49 — only ten numbers left. Clue 3: the
    ones digit is 4 + 2 = 6. So 46. Write the numbers 40-49 and cross them out
    as you go. Then let the child write a riddle for you.

D2. Put the numbers 1 to 9 in the square so that every row, every column and
    both diagonals add up to 15. Some are done for you.
        +----+----+----+
        | __ |  7 | __ |
        +----+----+----+
        | __ |  5 | __ |
        +----+----+----+
        |  4 | __ |  8 |
        +----+----+----+
    Answer: Top row 2, 7, 6; middle row 9, 5, 1; bottom row 4, 3, 8.
    Teacher explains: Start with a line that has only one gap. The middle
    column has 7, 5 and a gap: 15 − 12 = 3. The diagonal 4, 5, gap: 15 − 9 = 6
    (top right). Now the top row has 7 and 6, so the top left is 15 − 13 = 2.
    Keep going: always find a line with just one gap. Check every row, column
    and diagonal at the end. 5 is always in the middle of this magic square.

D3. In a number pyramid, each brick is the sum of the two bricks under it.
    Fill in the pyramid.
            [__]
          [__][__]
        [ 3][ 5][ 2]
    Answer: Middle row: 8 and 7. Top: 15.
    Teacher explains: Show it with real bricks or boxes. 3 + 5 = 8 goes on top
    of 3 and 5; 5 + 2 = 7 goes on top of 5 and 2. Then 8 + 7 = 15 at the top.
    Then try one with a missing brick at the bottom: if the top is 20 and the
    middle bricks are 12 and 8, the child must think backwards (take away).

D4. Solve the 4 × 4 sudoku. Every row, every column and every 2 × 2 box must
    have 1, 2, 3 and 4 exactly once.
        +-----+-----+
        | 1 _ | _ 4 |
        | _ 4 | 1 _ |
        +-----+-----+
        | 2 _ | _ 3 |
        | _ 3 | 2 _ |
        +-----+-----+
    Answer: Rows: 1 2 3 4 / 3 4 1 2 / 2 1 4 3 / 4 3 2 1.
    Teacher explains: Look for a row, column or box that is missing only one
    or two numbers. Row 1 has 1 and 4, so it needs 2 and 3. Column 2 already
    has 4 and 3, so row 1 column 2 cannot be 3: it is 2, and the other gap is
    3. Explain each step with "it must be ... because ...". Then let the child
    solve one alone.

D5. Ria is taller than Sonu. Sonu is taller than Tia. Who is the tallest? Who
    is the shortest?
    Answer: Ria is the tallest and Tia is the shortest.
    Teacher explains: Draw three sticks: put Ria higher than Sonu, then Tia
    lower than Sonu. Order: Ria, Sonu, Tia. Point out: we never compared Ria
    and Tia directly, but we still KNOW Ria is taller, because Ria is taller
    than Sonu and Sonu is taller than Tia. Act it out with three children
    standing in order.

D6. Amit, Bela and Chetan each like a different fruit: mango, apple or banana.
    Amit does not like mango. Bela likes banana. Who likes which fruit?
    Answer: Amit: apple. Bela: banana. Chetan: mango.
    Teacher explains: Draw a grid: names down the side, fruits across the top.
    Tick Bela-banana, and cross out banana for the others. Cross out
    Amit-mango. Now Amit has only apple left: tick it. Chetan gets the last
    fruit, mango. A grid of ticks and crosses keeps our thinking tidy.

D7. Is this true: "When you add two numbers, the answer is always bigger than
    both numbers"?
    Answer: Sometimes true. 3 + 4 = 7 is bigger than both, but 6 + 0 = 6 is
    not bigger than 6.
    Teacher explains: To show a statement is NOT always true, we need just ONE
    example where it fails (a counter-example). Let the child try many sums,
    then give a hint: "What about adding 0?" Teach the three answers: always
    true, sometimes true, never true.

D8. "If a number ends in 0, then it is even." 70 ends in 0. Is 70 even? Is
    every even number a number that ends in 0?
    Answer: Yes, 70 is even. No — 12, 34 and 58 are even but do not end in 0.
    Teacher explains: An if-then rule works one way. Numbers ending in 0 are
    all even, but the even numbers also end in 2, 4, 6 or 8. Draw a big circle
    "even numbers" with a smaller circle inside "ends in 0". Everything in the
    small circle is in the big one, but not the other way round.

D9. Which is the odd one out: 12, 18, 25, 30? Why?
    Answer: 25, because it is the only odd number (the others are even).
    Another good answer: 30, because it is the only one in the 10s count.
    Teacher explains: There can be more than one right answer if the reason is
    true. Ask "Why?" every time — the reason is the maths. Let the child make
    their own odd-one-out puzzles for the family.

D10. In this grid each number in the last column is the sum of the two numbers
     in its row, and each number in the bottom row is the sum of the column
     above. Find the missing numbers.
            4    5  |  __
            3   __  |   9
           ---------+-----
           __   __  |  __
     Answer: Row 1: 4 + 5 = 9. Row 2: 3 + 6 = 9. Bottom row: 7, 11, 18.
     Teacher explains: Fill a gap only when its row or column has just one
     gap. Row 2: 3 + ? = 9, so 6. Column 1: 4 + 3 = 7. Column 2: 5 + 6 = 11.
     Corner: 7 + 11 = 18, and check: 9 + 9 = 18 too. When two ways give the
     same answer, we know it is right.

D11. There are 3 coins that look the same, but one is a fake and is a little
     heavier. You may use a balance only ONCE. How can you find the fake?
     Answer: Put one coin on each side and keep the third coin out. If one
     side goes down, that coin is the fake. If they balance, the coin you kept
     out is the fake.
     Teacher explains: Act it out with a toy balance (or a ruler on a pencil)
     and three coins, one with a little clay stuck under it. Talk through
     every possible result: left down, right down, or level. A good plan works
     in EVERY case. That is logical thinking.

D12. I am thinking of a number from 1 to 20. You may ask questions that I
     answer only "yes" or "no". What is a clever first question?
     Answer: "Is it more than 10?" — it cuts the numbers in half. With halving
     questions, you can always find the number in 5 questions or fewer.
     Teacher explains: Compare a slow question ("Is it 7?" — usually no, and
     only one number is crossed out) with a halving question (10 numbers
     crossed out). 20 → 10 → 5 → 3 → 2 → 1: at most 5 questions. Play the game
     often and let the child be the one who asks.

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Who am I?
    a) I am between 20 and 30. I am even. My digits add up to 6.
    b) I am 10 more than 35.
    c) I am a 2-digit number in the 10s count. My tens digit is 4.
    d) I am between 70 and 80. My two digits are the same.
    e) I am a 2-digit number. My tens digit is 8 and my digits add to 9.
    f) I am between 30 and 40. You say me when counting in 5s. I am odd.
    g) I am double 18.
    h) I am between 10 and 20. My ones digit is 3 times my tens digit.
    i) I am half of 50.
    j) I am a 2-digit number. My tens digit is 1 more than my ones digit. My
       digits add to 7.
    k) I am the biggest 2-digit number whose digits add to 5.
    l) I am the smallest 2-digit odd number.
    m) I am 1 less than 100 and I am odd.
    n) I am between 45 and 55. My ones digit is 0.
    Answers: a) 24   b) 45   c) 40   d) 77   e) 81   f) 35   g) 36   h) 13
             i) 25   j) 43   k) 50   l) 11   m) 99   n) 50

M2. Magic squares: use 1 to 9 once each. Every row, column and diagonal adds
    up to 15. Answers are given row by row.
    a) +----+----+----+   b) +----+----+----+   c) +----+----+----+
       |  6 | __ | __ |      | __ |  9 | __ |      | __ | __ |  4 |
       +----+----+----+      +----+----+----+      +----+----+----+
       | __ |  5 | __ |      | __ |  5 | __ |      |  7 |  5 | __ |
       +----+----+----+      +----+----+----+      +----+----+----+
       | __ |  3 | __ |      | __ | __ |  6 |      | __ | __ | __ |
       +----+----+----+      +----+----+----+      +----+----+----+
    d) +----+----+----+   e) +----+----+----+   f) +----+----+----+
       |  8 | __ | __ |      | __ |  3 | __ |      | __ | __ |  4 |
       +----+----+----+      +----+----+----+      +----+----+----+
       | __ | __ |  7 |      |  9 | __ | __ |      | __ |  5 | __ |
       +----+----+----+      +----+----+----+      +----+----+----+
       | __ |  9 | __ |      | __ | __ |  6 |      |  6 |  7 | __ |
       +----+----+----+      +----+----+----+      +----+----+----+
    Answers:
      a) 6 7 2 / 1 5 9 / 8 3 4
      b) 4 9 2 / 3 5 7 / 8 1 6
      c) 2 9 4 / 7 5 3 / 6 1 8
      d) 8 1 6 / 3 5 7 / 4 9 2
      e) 4 3 8 / 9 5 1 / 2 7 6
      f) 8 3 4 / 1 5 9 / 6 7 2

M3. More magic squares. Use each number once.
    a) Use 2 to 10, total 18:          b) Use 11 to 19, total 45:
       +----+----+----+                   +----+----+----+
       |  3 | __ | __ |                   | __ | 17 | __ |
       +----+----+----+                   +----+----+----+
       | 10 |  6 | __ |                   | __ | 15 | 11 |
       +----+----+----+                   +----+----+----+
       | __ | __ |  9 |                   | 14 | __ | __ |
       +----+----+----+                   +----+----+----+
    c) Use 2, 4, 6 ... 18, total 30:
       +----+----+----+
       |  4 | __ | 12 |
       +----+----+----+
       | __ | 10 | __ |
       +----+----+----+
       | __ |  6 | __ |
       +----+----+----+
    Answers:
      a) 3 8 7 / 10 6 2 / 5 4 9
      b) 12 17 16 / 19 15 11 / 14 13 18
      c) 4 14 12 / 18 10 2 / 8 6 16

M4. Number pyramids: each brick is the sum of the two bricks under it. Fill in
    every brick (answers from the top row down).
    a)     [__]           b)     [__]           c)     [20]
         [__][__]              [__][__]              [12][__]
       [ 4][ 6][ 3]          [10][ 5][ 7]          [ 7][__][__]
    d)     [__]           e)       [__]         f)       [__]
         [15][18]                [__][__]              [__][__]
       [__][ 9][__]            [__][__][__]          [__][__][__]
                             [ 1][ 2][ 3][ 4]      [ 5][ 0][ 5][10]
    g)       [50]         h)       [__]
           [__][__]              [30][__]
         [__][12][13]          [__][16][11]
       [__][__][__][ 6]      [__][__][ 4][__]
    Answers:
      a) 19 / 10 9 / 4 6 3
      b) 27 / 15 12 / 10 5 7
      c) 20 / 12 8 / 7 5 3
      d) 33 / 15 18 / 6 9 9
      e) 20 / 8 12 / 3 5 7 / 1 2 3 4
      f) 30 / 10 20 / 5 5 15 / 5 0 5 10
      g) 50 / 25 25 / 13 12 13 / 8 5 7 6
      h) 57 / 30 27 / 14 16 11 / 2 12 4 7

M5. 4 × 4 sudoku: 1, 2, 3, 4 once in every row, column and 2 × 2 box. (Answers
    row by row.)
    a) +-----+-----+   b) +-----+-----+   c) +-----+-----+
       | _ _ | 4 _ |      | _ _ | _ _ |      | _ 1 | _ 4 |
       | 1 4 | 3 2 |      | _ _ | 4 1 |      | _ 4 | _ 3 |
       +-----+-----+      +-----+-----+      +-----+-----+
       | _ _ | 2 _ |      | _ 4 | 1 2 |      | _ 3 | 4 _ |
       | 3 _ | 1 _ |      | 1 _ | 3 _ |      | _ _ | _ _ |
       +-----+-----+      +-----+-----+      +-----+-----+
    Answers:
      a) 2 3 4 1 / 1 4 3 2 / 4 1 2 3 / 3 2 1 4
      b) 4 1 2 3 / 2 3 4 1 / 3 4 1 2 / 1 2 3 4
      c) 3 1 2 4 / 2 4 1 3 / 1 3 4 2 / 4 2 3 1

M6. Addition grids: each box = the number at the top of its column + the
    number at the start of its row. Fill every gap.
    a)   + |   3   5   2   b)   + |  10  20   5   c)   + |   7   8   9
         --+------------        --+------------        --+------------
         4 |   7  __   6        6 |  16  __  11       10 |  17  __  19
         1 |   4   6  __       30 |  40  50  __        2 |   9  10  __
    d)   + |  __   6   4   e)   + |   5  __   9
         --+------------        --+------------
         3 |   8  __  __       __ |   7  10  __
        __ |  __  11  __        7 |  __  __  __
    Answers:
      a) 9, 3
      b) 26, 35
      c) 18, 11
      d) tops 5, 6, 4; sides 3, 5; grid 8 9 7 / 10 11 9
      e) tops 5, 8, 9; sides 2, 7; grid 7 10 11 / 12 15 16

M7. Always, sometimes or never true?
    a) even + even is even
    b) odd + odd is odd
    c) a number + 0 = the same number
    d) adding two numbers gives more than both
    e) a 2-digit number is more than a 1-digit number
    f) a number ending in 5 is odd
    g) a number ending in 0 is odd
    h) numbers in the 5s count end in 5
    i) taking away makes a number smaller
    j) a shape with 4 sides is a square
    k) double a whole number is even
    l) even + odd is even
    Answers: a) always   b) never (it is even)   c) always
             d) sometimes (not with 0)   e) always   f) always   g) never
             h) sometimes (or 0)   i) sometimes (not with 0)   j) sometimes
             k) always   l) never (it is odd)

M8. Odd one out. Give the reason. (Other answers are fine if the reason is
    true.)
    a) 12, 18, 25, 30
    b) 15, 25, 35, 42
    c) 20, 40, 55, 70
    d) 3 + 4, 5 + 2, 6 + 1, 4 + 4
    e) 10 − 3, 9 − 2, 8 − 1, 9 − 3
    f) 11, 22, 33, 43
    g) 2, 4, 7, 8
    h) 100, 200, 250, 300
    i) 36, 63, 45, 27, 50
    j) 6, 9, 12, 16
    k) triangle, square, circle, pentagon
    l) cube, cuboid, sphere, square pyramid
    Answers:
      a) 25 (odd)
      b) 42 (not in the 5s count)
      c) 55 (not in the 10s count)
      d) 4 + 4 (8; the others are 7)
      e) 9 − 3 (6; the others are 7)
      f) 43 (digits not the same)
      g) 7 (odd)
      h) 250 (not whole hundreds)
      i) 50 (digits do not add to 9)
      j) 16 (not in the 3s count)
      k) circle (no straight sides)
      l) sphere (no flat face)

M9. Put in order.
    a) A is taller than B. B is taller than C. Tallest to shortest?
    b) P is older than Q. R is older than P. Oldest to youngest?
    c) X costs more than Y. Z costs less than Y. Most to least?
    d) K is faster than L but slower than M. Fastest to slowest?
    e) A is taller than B. C is shorter than B. D is taller than A. Tallest to
       shortest?
    f) In a line, W is ahead of X, X is ahead of Y, Z is behind Y. First to
       last?
    g) E weighs more than F. G weighs more than E. H weighs less than F.
       Heaviest to lightest?
    Answers: a) A, B, C   b) R, P, Q   c) X, Y, Z   d) M, K, L   e) D, A, B, C
             f) W, X, Y, Z   g) G, E, F, H

M10. Logic grids: who has what? (Draw a grid of ticks and crosses.)
     a) A, B, C each have a different one of ●, ■, ▲. A does not have ●. B has
        ■.
     b) P, Q, R, S each have a different number 1, 2, 3, 4. P's number is even.
        Q's number is 1 more than P's. R's number is more than S's.
     c) X, Y, Z hold 10, 20 and 30 (one each). X holds more than Y. Z holds the
        least.
     d) D, E, F each have a different one of ●, ■, ▲. D and F do not have ▲. F
        does not have ●.
     e) J, K, L, M each have a different number: 2, 5, 7, 9. K's number is even.
        J's number is more than M's. L's number is 2 more than J's.
     Answers: a) A ▲, B ■, C ●   b) P 2, Q 3, R 4, S 1   c) X 30, Y 20, Z 10
              d) D ●, E ▲, F ■   e) J 7, K 2, L 9, M 5

M11. Each shape stands for a number. The same shape is always the same number.
     Find the shapes.
     a) ▲ + ▲ = 10                          b) ● + ● + ● = 12
     c) ■ + 7 = 15                          d) ▲ = 5 and ▲ + ● = 12
     e) ● + ● = 14 and ● + ■ = 10           f) ■ − 6 = 9
     g) ▲ = 6 and ▲ + ▲ + ● = 20            h) ● + ■ = 10 and ● − ■ = 4
     i) ▲ + ▲ = ● and ● + ▲ = 15            j) ■ + ■ + ■ + ■ = 20
     k) 20 − ● = 12                         l) ● + ▲ = 9, ▲ + ■ = 11, ● + ■ = 12
     Answers: a) ▲ = 5   b) ● = 4   c) ■ = 8   d) ● = 7   e) ● = 7, ■ = 3
              f) ■ = 15   g) ● = 8   h) ● = 7, ■ = 3   i) ▲ = 5, ● = 10
              j) ■ = 5   k) ● = 8   l) ● = 5, ▲ = 4, ■ = 7

M12. Find all the ways. Make an organised list so nothing is missed.
     a) largest and smallest 2-digit numbers from 3, 8
     b) largest and smallest 3-digit numbers from 3, 7, 1
     c) largest and smallest 3-digit numbers from 5, 0, 9
     d) all 2-digit numbers from 1, 2, 3 (no digit used twice)
     e) all 2-digit numbers from 4, 6 (digits may repeat)
     f) ways A, B, C can stand in a line
     g) pairs from 2 shirts (S, T) and 3 shorts (1, 2, 3)
     h) handshakes when 3 people each shake hands once
     i) handshakes when 4 people each shake hands once
     j) ways to make 10 as ● + ■ with ● and ■ from 1 to 9
     Answers:
       a) 83, 38
       b) 731, 137
       c) 950, 509
       d) 12, 13, 21, 23, 31, 32 (6 numbers)
       e) 44, 46, 64, 66
       f) 6: ABC, ACB, BAC, BCA, CAB, CBA
       g) 6: S1, S2, S3, T1, T2, T3
       h) 3
       i) 6
       j) 9: 1 + 9, 2 + 8, 3 + 7, 4 + 6, 5 + 5, 6 + 4, 7 + 3, 8 + 2, 9 + 1

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: use real objects — number cards to move around in a magic square
  or sudoku, bricks for pyramids, children standing in order for "taller than"
  puzzles. Give one clue at a time and let the child cross out what it rules
  out. Praise good reasons even when the answer is wrong. Harder: make up
  riddles, pyramids and odd-one-out puzzles for the family; solve a magic
  square with only the 5 given; try a 4 × 4 sudoku with only 4 numbers given;
  play Nim (take 1 or 2 sticks from 10; who takes the last loses) and look for
  a winning plan.


================================================================================
TOPIC 3.32 — ABACUS / MENTAL ARITHMETIC PRACTICE (OPTIONAL, BUILDS CALCULATION
             FLUENCY)
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - This topic is OPTIONAL. It builds quick, confident calculation for
    children who enjoy it. Use a real abacus (a soroban has 1 upper bead and 4
    lower beads on each rod).
  - Know the parts: frame, rods, the bar (beam). An upper bead pushed down to
    the bar is worth 5; each lower bead pushed up to the bar is worth 1. Beads
    away from the bar count nothing. In this file ● means a bead touching the
    bar (it counts) and ○ a bead away from the bar.
  - The rightmost rod is ones, then tens, then hundreds. Show and read any
    number from 0 to 999.
  - SMALL FRIENDS (pairs that make 5): 1 and 4, 2 and 3. To add 4 when there
    is no room: add 5 and take away 1. To take away 3 when there are not
    enough lower beads: take away 5 and add 2.
  - BIG FRIENDS (pairs that make 10): 1 and 9, 2 and 8, 3 and 7, 4 and 6, 5
    and 5. To add 7 when it does not fit: take away 3 and add 10. To take away
    8: take away 10 and add 2.
  - Mental arithmetic: add and subtract chains of single digits
    (7 + 5 − 3 + 8), add lists of digits quickly by finding pairs that make
    10, and double numbers rapidly.
  - Later, "see" the abacus in your head and move imaginary beads.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. What is an abacus, and how do we show 7 on one rod?
          ●      <- upper bead (5), touching the bar
        ━━━━━
          ●      <- lower bead (1), touching the bar
          ●      <- lower bead (1), touching the bar
          ○
          ○
    Answer: An abacus is a frame with rods and beads for counting. On a
    soroban each rod has 1 upper bead worth 5 and 4 lower beads worth 1 each.
    7 = push the upper bead down (5) and 2 lower beads up (1 + 1). 5 + 2 = 7.
    Teacher explains: Show the child the bar across the middle. Only beads
    touching the bar count. Clear the abacus (all beads away from the bar) and
    say "zero". Count 1, 2, 3, 4 with lower beads. For 5, the four lower beads
    go down and the upper bead comes down: "one 5 instead of five 1s". Then 6
    is 5 and 1, 7 is 5 and 2 ... 9 is 5 and 4. Let the child show every number
    0 to 9.

D2. Show 36 on the abacus.
          T   O
          ○   ●
         ━━━━━━━
          ●   ●
          ●   ○
          ●   ○
          ○   ○
    Answer: Tens rod: 3 lower beads (30). Ones rod: the upper bead and 1 lower
    bead (6).
    Teacher explains: Say the number in parts: 36 is 3 tens and 6 ones. Choose
    a rod for the ones (on the right) and the rod to its left for tens. Always
    set the tens first, then the ones, reading left to right like a book. Then
    read it back: "3 on the tens rod is 30, 6 on the ones rod, 36."

D3. What number does this abacus show?
          H   T   O
          ○   ●   ○
         ━━━━━━━━━━━
          ●   ●   ●
          ●   ●   ●
          ●   ●   ○
          ●   ○   ○
    Answer: 482.
    Teacher explains: Read one rod at a time from the left. Hundreds rod: 4
    lower beads = 4, so 400. Tens rod: upper bead and 3 lower = 8, so 80. Ones
    rod: 2 lower = 2. 400 + 80 + 2 = 482. Then let the child set a number for
    you to read.

D4. The ones rod shows 3. How do you add 4 on the abacus?
    Answer: 3 + 4 = 7. There is only 1 free lower bead, so bring down the
    upper bead (+5) and take away 1 lower bead (−1). The rod now shows
    5 + 2 = 7.
    Teacher explains: 4 and 1 are SMALL FRIENDS: they make 5. Adding 4 is the
    same as adding 5 and taking away 1. Say it as you move: "plus 4 is plus 5,
    minus 1." Practise every small-friend move: +4 = +5 −1, +3 = +5 −2, +2 =
    +5 −3, +1 = +5 −4.

D5. The ones rod shows 6. How do you take away 3?
    Answer: 6 − 3 = 3. There is only 1 lower bead to take, so take away the
    upper bead (−5) and push up 2 lower beads (+2). Now 3 lower beads show 3.
    Teacher explains: Taking away 3 is the same as taking away 5 and giving
    back 2, because 3 and 2 are small friends. Say: "minus 3 is minus 5, plus
    2." Check: 6 − 5 = 1, 1 + 2 = 3.

D6. The abacus shows 8. How do you add 7?
    Answer: 8 + 7 = 15. 7 does not fit on the ones rod, so use its big friend
    3: take away 3 from the ones rod (8 − 3 = 5), then add 1 bead on the tens
    rod (+10). The abacus shows 15.
    Teacher explains: 3 and 7 are BIG FRIENDS: they make 10. Adding 7 is the
    same as adding 10 and taking away 3. Say: "plus 7 is minus 3, plus 10."
    Practise all big friends: 1 and 9, 2 and 8, 3 and 7, 4 and 6, 5 and 5.

D7. The abacus shows 13. How do you take away 6?
    Answer: 13 − 6 = 7. Take away 1 from the tens rod (−10) and add 4 to the
    ones rod (+4). The ones rod has 3, so adding 4 needs the small friend: +5
    −1. The ones rod shows 7, the tens rod 0: 7.
    Teacher explains: Taking away 6 is the same as taking away 10 and giving
    back 4 (6 and 4 are big friends). Sometimes a big friend and a small
    friend are both needed — move slowly and say each step aloud. Check with a
    number line: 13 back 3 is 10, back 3 more is 7.

D8. Work out in your head: 7 + 5 − 3 + 8.
    Answer: 17.
    Teacher explains: Do one step at a time and say only the running answer:
    7 + 5 = 12 (7 + 3 makes 10, and 2 more); 12 − 3 = 9; 9 + 8 = 17 (9 + 1
    makes 10, and 7 more). Start with short chains of 3 numbers, said slowly,
    then make them longer and faster. Children can check on the abacus.

D9. Add quickly: 4, 6, 3, 7, 5.
    Answer: 25.
    Teacher explains: Look for pairs that make 10 (big friends): 4 + 6 = 10
    and 3 + 7 = 10. That is 20, and 5 more is 25. Adding in any order gives
    the same total, so hunt for tens first. Then give lists like 8, 2, 9, 1, 6
    (26).

D10. Riya's grandfather puts ₹1 in her piggy bank in week 1 and doubles it
     every week: ₹2 in week 2, ₹4 in week 3. How much does he put in during
     week 7?
     Answer: ₹64. (1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64)
     Teacher explains: Doubling is adding a number to itself. Say the chain
     aloud: "1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64". For 16 + 16, double the tens (20) and
     double the ones (12): 32. For 32 + 32: 60 + 4 = 64. Doubling grows very
     fast! Practise doubles to 50 every day.

D11. Close your eyes and imagine an abacus showing 23. Add 14 on your
     imaginary abacus. What do you see?
     Answer: 37: the tens rod shows 3 (3 lower beads) and the ones rod shows 7
     (upper bead and 2 lower beads).
     Teacher explains: First do it on a real abacus while saying each move:
     "tens rod: push up 1 more bead, 3; ones rod: 3 and 4 — small friend, down
     the 5, take away 1: 7." Then do it with fingers moving in the air over a
     picture of an abacus, then with eyes closed. This picture in the head is
     what makes abacus children so quick.

D12. Add 45 + 38 on the abacus.
     Answer: 83.
     Teacher explains: Set 45. Add the tens first: tens rod 4 + 3. There is no
     free lower bead, so small friend: +5 −2, and the tens rod shows 7. Now
     the ones: 5 + 8. 8 does not fit, so big friend: −2 +10. Taking 2 from 5
     needs a small friend too (−5 +3), so the ones rod shows 3; then +10 makes
     the tens rod 8. Read: 83. Check mentally: 45 + 40 = 85, 85 − 2 = 83. Go
     slowly — this is a challenge question.

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Each rod is written as: upper bead | lower beads (● touches the bar and
    counts, ○ does not). What digit is it?
    a) ○ | ● ● ● ○   b) ● | ● ● ○ ○   c) ○ | ○ ○ ○ ○   d) ● | ○ ○ ○ ○
    e) ● | ● ● ● ●   f) ○ | ● ○ ○ ○   g) ● | ● ○ ○ ○   h) ○ | ● ● ● ●
    i) ● | ● ● ● ○   j) ○ | ● ● ○ ○
    Answers: a) 3   b) 7   c) 0   d) 5   e) 9   f) 1   g) 6   h) 4   i) 8   j) 2

M2. What number does each abacus show?
    a)   H   T   O    b)   H   T   O    c)   H   T   O    d)   H   T   O
         ○   ○   ●         ○   ○   ●         ○   ●   ●         ○   ●   ○
        ━━━━━━━━━━━       ━━━━━━━━━━━       ━━━━━━━━━━━       ━━━━━━━━━━━
         ○   ○   ●         ○   ●   ●         ○   ○   ●         ○   ●   ○
         ○   ○   ●         ○   ●   ○         ○   ○   ●         ○   ●   ○

M3. Which beads do you move to show each number? (H = hundreds rod, T = tens
    rod, O = ones rod.)
    a) 6     b) 9     c) 15    d) 27    e) 40    f) 55    g) 83
    h) 99    i) 100   j) 347   k) 507   l) 768   m) 999   n) 620
    Answers:
      a) H none; T none; O upper + 1 lower
      b) H none; T none; O upper + 4 lower
      c) H none; T 1 lower; O upper
      d) H none; T 2 lower; O upper + 2 lower
      e) H none; T 4 lower; O none
      f) H none; T upper; O upper
      g) H none; T upper + 3 lower; O 3 lower
      h) H none; T upper + 4 lower; O upper + 4 lower
      i) H 1 lower; T none; O none
      j) H 3 lower; T 4 lower; O upper + 2 lower
      k) H upper; T none; O upper + 2 lower
      l) H upper + 2 lower; T upper + 1 lower; O upper + 3 lower
      m) H upper + 4 lower; T upper + 4 lower; O upper + 4 lower
      n) H upper + 1 lower; T 2 lower; O none

M4. Small friends make 5. Big friends make 10. Fill in.
    a) small friend of 1   b) small friend of 2   c) small friend of 3
    d) small friend of 4   e) big friend of 1     f) big friend of 2
    g) big friend of 3     h) big friend of 4     i) big friend of 5
    j) big friend of 6     k) big friend of 7     l) big friend of 8
    m) big friend of 9     n) +4 = +5 − __        o) +3 = +5 − __
    p) −2 = −5 + __        q) +6 = −__ + 10       r) +9 = −__ + 10
    s) −7 = −10 + __
    Answers: a) 4   b) 3   c) 2   d) 1   e) 9   f) 8   g) 7   h) 6   i) 5   j) 4
             k) 3   l) 2   m) 1   n) 1   o) 2   p) 3   q) 4   r) 1   s) 3

M5. Add on one rod using a SMALL FRIEND (+5, then take away the friend). Write
    the moves and the answer.
    a) 1 + 4   b) 2 + 3   c) 2 + 4   d) 3 + 2   e) 3 + 3
    f) 3 + 4   g) 4 + 1   h) 4 + 2   i) 4 + 3   j) 4 + 4
    Answers: a) +5 −1 = 5   b) +5 −2 = 5   c) +5 −1 = 6   d) +5 −3 = 5
             e) +5 −2 = 6   f) +5 −1 = 7   g) +5 −4 = 5   h) +5 −3 = 6
             i) +5 −2 = 7   j) +5 −1 = 8

M6. Take away on one rod using a SMALL FRIEND (−5, then add the friend). Write
    the moves and the answer.
    a) 5 − 1   b) 5 − 2   c) 5 − 3   d) 5 − 4   e) 6 − 2
    f) 6 − 3   g) 6 − 4   h) 7 − 3   i) 7 − 4   j) 8 − 4
    Answers: a) −5 +4 = 4   b) −5 +3 = 3   c) −5 +2 = 2   d) −5 +1 = 1
             e) −5 +3 = 4   f) −5 +2 = 3   g) −5 +1 = 2   h) −5 +2 = 4
             i) −5 +1 = 3   j) −5 +1 = 4

M7. Add using a BIG FRIEND (take away the friend, then add 10). Write the
    moves and the answer.
    a) 9 + 1    b) 8 + 2    c) 7 + 3    d) 6 + 4    e) 5 + 5
    f) 9 + 2    g) 8 + 3    h) 7 + 4    i) 6 + 5    j) 4 + 6
    k) 5 + 7    l) 3 + 8    m) 2 + 9    n) 8 + 6    o) 9 + 9
    p) 7 + 7    q) 18 + 7   r) 26 + 5   s) 37 + 4   t) 49 + 3
    Answers: a) −9 +10 = 10   b) −8 +10 = 10   c) −7 +10 = 10   d) −6 +10 = 10
             e) −5 +10 = 10   f) −8 +10 = 11   g) −7 +10 = 11   h) −6 +10 = 11
             i) −5 +10 = 11   j) −4 +10 = 10   k) −3 +10 = 12   l) −2 +10 = 11
             m) −1 +10 = 11   n) −4 +10 = 14   o) −1 +10 = 18   p) −3 +10 = 14
             q) −3 +10 = 25   r) −5 +10 = 31   s) −6 +10 = 41   t) −7 +10 = 52

M8. Take away using a BIG FRIEND (take away 10, then add the friend). Write
    the moves and the answer.
    a) 10 − 1   b) 11 − 2   c) 12 − 3   d) 13 − 4   e) 14 − 5
    f) 15 − 6   g) 16 − 7   h) 17 − 8   i) 18 − 9   j) 12 − 9
    k) 13 − 7   l) 11 − 8   m) 14 − 6   n) 15 − 8   o) 16 − 9
    p) 23 − 5   q) 41 − 3   r) 52 − 6

             e) −10 +5 = 9   f) −10 +4 = 9   g) −10 +3 = 9   h) −10 +2 = 9
             i) −10 +1 = 9   j) −10 +1 = 3   k) −10 +3 = 6   l) −10 +2 = 3
             m) −10 +4 = 8   n) −10 +2 = 7   o) −10 +1 = 7   p) −10 +5 = 18
             q) −10 +7 = 38   r) −10 +4 = 46

M9. Work these out on the abacus (tens first, then ones). Then check in your
    head.

M10. Mental chains: work from left to right, saying only the running answer.
     a) 4 − 3 + 4 − 1               b) 1 + 9 − 8 + 1 + 3 + 8
     c) 5 + 4 − 6 + 9 + 3           d) 1 + 1 + 4 + 5 − 8 + 5 + 2
     e) 4 + 5 + 4 + 5 + 2 + 1       f) 3 + 2 + 3 + 1 − 3 + 4 − 8
     g) 6 + 8 + 4 + 9 − 4 + 7       h) 4 + 9 + 6 + 3 + 1 + 2
     i) 6 + 7 + 8 + 6 − 6           j) 3 + 5 − 6 + 1 + 3
     k) 4 + 9 − 7 + 7               l) 4 + 8 − 1 − 5 − 2
     m) 2 + 9 + 6 − 2               n) 4 + 9 − 2 + 1 + 4
     o) 9 − 6 + 1 + 6 + 9 − 3       p) 7 + 8 + 9 − 8 + 4
     Answers: a) 4   b) 14   c) 15   d) 10   e) 21   f) 2   g) 30   h) 25
              i) 21   j) 6   k) 13   l) 4   m) 15   n) 16   o) 16   p) 20

M11. Flash lists: add all the digits. Look for pairs that make 10 first.
     a) 1, 1, 2, 3            b) 9, 1, 4, 5            c) 1, 5, 9, 7
     d) 5, 7, 3, 4, 1         e) 7, 4, 8, 2, 1         f) 8, 6, 3, 6, 9
     g) 4, 3, 3, 9, 5, 5      h) 4, 3, 7, 9, 6, 1      i) 1, 2, 6, 6, 9, 7
     j) 3, 9, 9, 4, 7, 8, 7   k) 5, 4, 8, 8, 9, 4, 1   l) 7, 5, 7, 6, 5, 8, 6
     Answers: a) 7   b) 19   c) 22   d) 20   e) 22   f) 32   g) 29   h) 30
              i) 31   j) 47   k) 39   l) 44

M12. Rapid doubling. For m) to p) keep doubling.
     a) double 6                           b) double 7
     c) double 8                           d) double 9
     e) double 12                          f) double 15
     g) double 16                          h) double 25
     i) double 35                          j) double 45
     k) double 50                          l) double 125
     m) 1 → 2 → __ → ... (7 doublings)     n) 3 → 6 → __ → ... (5 doublings)
     o) 5 → 10 → __ → ... (5 doublings)    p) 25 → 50 → __ → ... (5 doublings)
     Answers: a) 12   b) 14   c) 16   d) 18   e) 24   f) 30   g) 32   h) 50
              i) 70   j) 90   k) 100   l) 250
              m) 1 → 2 → 4 → 8 → 16 → 32 → 64 → 128
              n) 3 → 6 → 12 → 24 → 48 → 96   o) 5 → 10 → 20 → 40 → 80 → 160
              p) 25 → 50 → 100 → 200 → 400 → 800

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: spend a whole week only showing and reading numbers 0 to 9 on
  one rod, then 10 to 99 on two rods, before any adding. Practise the small
  friends with fingers (one hand: 5 = 1 + 4 = 2 + 3), and the big friends with
  ten-frames, before using them on the abacus. Keep sessions to 10 minutes.
  Harder: time a flash list and try to beat your own time (not a friend's); do
  chains with 2-digit numbers; move to "air abacus" and then eyes-closed
  mental work. The goal is quick, correct thinking — never let speed make the
  child anxious.


================================================================================
MIXED REVISION TEST — STAGE 3
================================================================================
Two parts with the same number of questions: Part A is descriptive (word
problems and "explain" questions, spoken or written), Part B is
mathematical only. 1 mark each, 50 marks in all. Spread it over a week.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE, 25 marks
  1.  Write four hundred six in numerals. Why do we need the 0?
  2.  In 482, what is the value of the 8? Explain using blocks.
  3.  Write 709 in expanded form and explain what each part means.
  4.  Which is greater, 358 or 385? Explain how you decided.
  5.  Is 6 + 4 = 5 + 5 true? Explain what "=" means.
  6.  On a number line from 0 to 100, where is 45? How did you find it?
  7.  How can knowing 8 + 8 = 16 help you work out 8 + 9?
  8.  Riya had 15 stickers and gave away 7. How many are left? Show the
      "think addition" way.
  9.  Write the fact family for 6, 8 and 14.
 10.  Explain why 7 + 5 + 3 is easy to add in your head.
 11.  A pencil costs ₹12. What do 2 pencils cost? What is half of ₹12?
 12.  A class has 34 girls and 25 boys. How many children are there?
 13.  A shop had 352 mangoes and sold 178. How many are left? Show the
      column working and explain the borrowing.
 14.  How would you work out 46 + 9 in your head?
 15.  About how much is 38 + 51? Round each number to the nearest 10.
 16.  Aman had ₹50. He bought a book for ₹28 and a pen for ₹12. How much
      money is left?
 17.  There are 4 plates with 5 laddoos on each. How many laddoos? Draw
      an array and write a multiplication.
 18.  24 children make teams of 4. How many teams? Is this sharing or
      grouping?
 19.  3 friends share 12 grapes equally. What fraction does each get, and
      how many grapes is that?
 20.  A 12 cm ribbon is cut into quarters. How long is each piece?
 21.  How many grams are in 1 kg? A bag holds 2 kg of rice. How many
      grams is that?
 22.  School starts at 8:30 and lunch is at 12:30. How long is it from
      the start of school to lunch?
 23.  You pay ₹100 for a toy that costs ₹65. How much change? Show the
      counting-up method.
 24.  You face North and turn a quarter turn clockwise. Which way do you
      face now? What about a half turn from there?
 25.  A bar graph shows: cricket 12, football 8, kabaddi 5 children. How
      many more like cricket than kabaddi? How many children in all?

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY, 25 marks
 26.  Write the number: 3 hundreds 0 tens 7 ones.
 27.  599, 600, __, __, 603
 28.  800 + 60 + 4 = __          __ = 500 + 9
 29.  Put < , > or =:  469 __ 496      700 __ 699      250 __ 200 + 50
 30.  7 + 8 = __ + 10            9 + 6 = 5 + __
 31.  6 + 7 = __   9 + 5 = __   8 + 4 = __   7 + 7 = __
 32.  15 − 8 = __  13 − 6 = __  17 − 9 = __  11 − 4 = __
 33.  __ + 9 = 16          20 − __ = 12
 34.  Double 35 = __       Half of 90 = __
 35.  53 + 24 = __         86 − 45 = __
 36.  67 + 48 = __         93 − 57 = __
 37.  458 + 276 = __       602 − 348 = __
 38.  74 + 19 = __ (add 20, take 1)
 39.  Round to the nearest 10:  34, 65, 297
 40.  4 × 3 = __   5 × 7 = __   10 × 6 = __   2 × 9 = __
 41.  3 × 8 = __   4 × 9 = __   3 × 6 = __    4 × 7 = __
 42.  35 ÷ 5 = __   28 ÷ 4 = __   21 ÷ 3 = __   60 ÷ 10 = __
 43.  1/2 of 18 = __   1/3 of 15 = __   1/4 of 20 = __
 44.  3 m = __ cm    1 kg = __ g    2 L = __ mL
 45.  Write the time: minute hand on 9, hour hand just before 4.
 46.  ₹20 + ₹10 + ₹5 + ₹2 = __
 47.  Faces, edges, vertices of a cube?
 48.  5, 10, 15, __, 25, __        100, 90, 80, __, __
 49.  Which is the odd one out: 12, 18, 25, 30? Why?
 50.  7 + 5 − 3 + 8 − 6 = __

ANSWER KEY
  Part A (accept any clear, correct explanation)
   1. 406; the 0 shows there are no tens (without it, 46 is a different
      number)
   2. 80 (8 tens = 8 ten-rods)
   3. 700 + 0 + 9, or 700 + 9: 7 hundreds, 0 tens, 9 ones
   4. 385; same hundreds (3), then compare tens: 8 tens > 5 tens
   5. true; both sides have the same value, 10
   6. halfway between 40 and 50
   7. 8 + 9 is one more than 8 + 8, so 17
   8. 8 stickers; think 7 + ? = 15, and 7 + 8 = 15
   9. 6 + 8 = 14, 8 + 6 = 14, 14 − 6 = 8, 14 − 8 = 6
  10. 7 + 3 = 10 first, then 10 + 5 = 15
  11. ₹24; ₹6
  12. 59 children
  13. 174 mangoes; borrow a ten (and a hundred) because 2 < 8 and then
      4 < 7 after borrowing
  14. e.g. 46 + 10 = 56, then take away 1: 55
  15. about 40 + 50 = 90 (exact answer 89)
  16. ₹10 (28 + 12 = 40; 50 − 40 = 10)
  17. 20 laddoos; 4 × 5 = 20 (4 rows of 5)
  18. 6 teams; grouping (we know the size of each group)
  19. one third (1/3) each, which is 4 grapes
  20. 3 cm
  21. 1000 g; 2000 g
  22. 4 hours
  23. ₹35 (65 → 70 is ₹5, 70 → 100 is ₹30)
  24. East; West
  25. 7 more; 25 children
  Part B
  26. 307                27. 601, 602           28. 864 ; 509
  29. < ; > ; =           30. 5 ; 10             31. 13, 14, 12, 14
  32. 7, 7, 8, 7          33. 7 ; 8              34. 70 ; 45
  35. 77 ; 41             36. 115 ; 36           37. 734 ; 254
  38. 93                 39. 30, 70, 300        40. 12, 35, 60, 18
  41. 24, 36, 18, 28      42. 7, 7, 7, 6         43. 9 ; 5 ; 5
  44. 300 ; 1000 ; 2000   45. quarter to 4 (3:45)
  46. ₹37                47. 6, 12, 8
  48. 20, 30 ; 70, 60    49. 25, the only odd number
  50. 11

Scoring guide (out of 50):
  43-50  Ready for Stage 4.
  32-42  Nearly there — practise the topics of the missed questions for
         two weeks, then try those questions again.
  0-31   Keep working through Stage 3 (revise Stage 2 if needed); test
         again in a month.
  Compare the two parts: strong in B but weak in A means the child needs
  more word problems and explaining; strong in A but weak in B means more
  fact and drill practice for speed and accuracy.


================================================================================
END OF STAGE 3 — READINESS CHECKLIST FOR STAGE 4
================================================================================
The child is ready for Stage 4 (Grades 3-4) when they can usually:
  [ ] Read, write, count and order numbers to 1000, in numerals and words
  [ ] Explain the value of each digit (hundreds, tens, ones) and use zero
      as a placeholder
  [ ] Write numbers in expanded form and back
  [ ] Compare numbers using <, > and =
  [ ] Understand "=" as "the same value as" and solve balance puzzles
  [ ] Place numbers on a number line and make jumps on it
  [ ] Recall addition facts to 20 and their subtraction facts quickly
  [ ] Write fact families and find missing numbers in any position
  [ ] Add numbers in any order and group them cleverly (make 10s)
  [ ] Double and halve numbers to 100; know the rules for 0 and 1
  [ ] Add and subtract 2- and 3-digit numbers, with and without
      regrouping, and check answers
  [ ] Use mental strategies (add 10, compensate, bridge through 10)
  [ ] Round to the nearest 10 (and 100) and estimate answers
  [ ] Solve one-step and two-step word problems and explain the method
  [ ] Explain multiplication as equal groups and arrays, and division as
      sharing and grouping
  [ ] Know the 2, 3, 4, 5 and 10 times tables and their division facts
  [ ] Find 1/2, 1/3 and 1/4 of shapes, sets and lengths
  [ ] Measure in cm and m; weigh in g and kg; measure capacity in mL and L
  [ ] Tell time to 5 minutes; use a calendar
  [ ] Count money, add prices and give change
  [ ] Describe 2D and 3D shapes by faces, edges, vertices, sides and angles
  [ ] Use quarter, half and full turns, clockwise and anticlockwise
  [ ] Continue and explain number patterns and hundred-chart patterns
  [ ] Read and make pictographs and bar graphs, and answer questions
  [ ] Solve simple logic puzzles and explain the reasoning

Multi-digit multiplication, long division, fractions of amounts,
decimals, perimeter and area are the main new work of Stage 4.
================================================================================
